CSSBB Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Brief the sponsor on the cost of delay and the resources and decisions needed): The sponsor owns the charter, the resources, and the removal of organizational barriers, so a project stalled on all three needs the sponsor engaged in those duties. Framing the gaps as a cost of delay gives the sponsor a business reason to act, which is how sponsorship is meant to work in a deployment. The options that move ownership of the project somewhere else leave the same gaps open. Continued data collection does not supply the decisions the team is waiting on.
  2. The correct answer is A (The method was developed at Motorola and later spread widely after adoption at General Electric): Six Sigma started at Motorola in the middle 1980s, where Bill Smith connected defect reduction to cost, and it became widely known after General Electric adopted it under Jack Welch. Bell Labs is linked to Shewhart's early work in statistical quality control, not to the origin of Six Sigma. The Japanese Union of Scientists and Engineers is connected with the Deming Prize and Japanese total quality practice. The option that credits General Electric with the origin reverses the two companies that matter most in the story.
  3. The correct answer is D (Link project selection to strategic goals and assign a champion and sponsor to each): Deployment produces business results when projects are selected from the strategic plan and each one has executive ownership, because those two elements tie the improvement effort to goals the business already tracks. Training and newsletters build capability and interest, but neither one chooses a project that touches a customer critical process. Lowering the approval threshold increases the number of small projects without repairing the missing link to strategy. A portfolio with named champions and sponsors also gives leadership a way to review progress and move resources.
  4. The correct answer is B (Use a strategy map and scorecard so projects trace to goals, owners, and review dates): A strategy map shows how improvement work connects to the goals in the business plan, and a scorecard adds a measure, an owner, and a review date for each initiative. That combination is how an organization deploys policy and follows the portfolio through the year. A quarterly list of finished projects reports history rather than alignment. Department level selection and ranking by tool count both break the link between the portfolio and the business plan.
  5. The correct answer is B (Run a competitive benchmark against the best performer and study the practices behind the gap): Benchmarking compares the process with a strong performer, then studies how that performer achieves the result so the practice can be adapted for this center. The competitor figure alone sets an aspiration, and the study of practices is what turns the gap into a plan. A customer survey measures satisfaction rather than the process capability that produces answer speed. Comparing only with the center's own history sets an incremental target and leaves the external gap in place.
  6. The correct answer is D (A stakeholder analysis that records each group's needs, influence, and measures of success): A stakeholder analysis lists each group, what it needs from the change, how much influence it holds, and how success will be measured for it, which is exactly the view the team was asked to produce. A control chart looks at process variation over time and says nothing about the three groups. Force field analysis examines the pressures for and against a change but does not organize needs by group. A failure mode analysis studies how a change could fail rather than who is affected by it.
  7. The correct answer is A (The first is competitive benchmarking and the second is functional benchmarking across industries): Comparing with direct peers in the same sector is competitive benchmarking, and studying a leading organization in a different industry is functional benchmarking. The value of the second study comes from a process that is not a direct competitor but performs the same function well. Internal benchmarking compares units inside one organization, which neither study does. Process benchmarking is not the label that separates a peer comparison from a cross industry one.
  8. The correct answer is C (Cost of poor quality, which totals the internal and external failure costs of a family): Scrap and rework are internal failure costs and warranty claims are external failure costs, so the three items together form the cost of poor quality for that family. Grouping them into one figure and comparing it with sales is the standard way to size an improvement opportunity. Inspection labor would count as appraisal instead, which belongs with the cost of good quality. Return on investment and net present value are capital measures, and takt time is a pace measure with no cost content.
  9. The correct answer is B (Select members with process knowledge and define each role, then confirm time commitments): A cross functional team works when the members know the process, understand their role, and have time set aside for the work, because those three elements decide whether the team can meet and settle anything. Members drawn only from one manager's group bring a narrow view of a process that spans three departments. Inviting every stakeholder creates a group too large to make decisions. Waiting for volunteers delays the start and leaves the skill mix to chance.
  10. The correct answer is D (Use round robin brainstorming first, then a nominal group vote to rank the ideas): Round robin gives each person a turn, which draws out the quiet members, and the nominal group vote then ranks the ideas in a way the whole team can see. Combining the two covers both the idea gathering and the decision. Excluding members or moving the discussion to email removes the exchange that builds ownership. A single speaking rule without a structured decision step leaves the group with no way to choose.
  11. The correct answer is A (The team is storming, so the leader should clarify roles and work through the conflict openly): Disagreement about who owns a task and a member who withdraws are the marks of storming, and the way through is to clarify responsibilities and surface the conflict rather than let it sit. Forming comes before any of this and is marked by politeness and dependence on the leader. Performing is a later stage of settled working relationships. Adjourning is the closing of a team that has finished its work.
  12. The correct answer is C (Train the Green Belt on the specific tests and charts the project will use, when needed): Team training works best when it is tied to the tools the project will actually use and delivered close to the point of need, because that is when the skill gets applied. A general communication workshop does not cover the hypothesis test or the control chart this project requires. Running the analysis away from the team removes the learning and leaves the team unable to explain the result. A full statistics certificate delays the project well beyond its scope.
  13. The correct answer is C (Use multi-voting to narrow the list, then build consensus around the leading cause): Multi-voting narrows a list of choices in a visible way, and the consensus step then gives the group a decision its members will support when the improve phase begins. That support matters because the team will have to act on the cause it chooses. Letting the most senior member decide trades commitment for speed. Averaging three causes or splitting them across subteams delays the choice and spreads the effort.
  14. The correct answer is A (Translate the customer statements into CTQs with targets and tolerances the team can measure): Voice of the customer work turns what customers say into critical to quality requirements, and each requirement needs a measure, a target, and a tolerance so the team can tell whether it was met. Fast page loads and a rarely mentioned report both pass through that translation step, where their relative weight becomes visible. Counting how often a statement appears measures frequency rather than importance. Confirming statements with sales adds a filter, and adding features to a release plan skips the requirement step.
  15. The correct answer is D (State the measurable problem, the process in scope, the goal, and the completion date): A charter becomes usable when the problem is stated in measurable terms, the process boundary is drawn, the goal is quantified, and the end date is set, since those four items let the team start and let the sponsor judge the result. A statement that tools will be used says nothing about the problem or the boundary. Member names and a meeting schedule are planning details. A summary of complaints is background rather than a measurable problem.
  16. The correct answer is B (The project can save about 140,000 dollars a year, which repays the effort quickly): Half of 280,000 dollars is 140,000 dollars, so the annual benefit of the project is 140,000 dollars, and comparing that figure with the cost of the project is what makes the business case. The other statements do not quantify the return. Saying that errors are visible to customers describes the problem rather than the benefit. Reassigning the order entry team is a staffing idea, and waiting for the industry average sets no financial target.
  17. The correct answer is D (A critical path analysis that shows the longest path and the float on each task): Critical path analysis finds the longest chain of dependent tasks, which sets the shortest possible schedule, and it reports the float on each task so the team can see what can slip without moving the finish date. A Pareto chart ranks counts by size and does not model sequence. A responsibility matrix shows who does what. A process map shows the flow as the customer experiences it but does not compute the shortest schedule.
  18. The correct answer is B (An affinity diagram that groups the statements, followed by a prioritization matrix): With no data collected yet, the team is working from statements, and an affinity diagram groups related complaints into themes while a prioritization matrix ranks those themes by importance and effort. That pairing turns a long list into a short one the team can act on. A control chart, a capability study, and a designed experiment all need process measurements that do not exist yet.
  19. The correct answer is C (The team members, their assigned roles, and the resources committed): The extract carries a business case, a measured problem statement, a goal with a date, scope boundaries, and milestones, but it never names the people on the team, what each one is responsible for, or the resources the project can draw on. A charter without named members and committed resources leaves the team unable to start the work or to know how much time and support it has. The problem statement, the scope, and the milestones are all present in the extract, so the options that name them describe what the panel already shows.
  20. The correct answer is A (30 seconds per unit, from 440 available minutes divided by 880 units): Available time is the 480 minutes in the shift less the 40 minutes lost to breaks and meetings, which leaves 440 minutes. Takt time is available time divided by customer demand, so 440 divided by 880 is 0.5 minutes per unit, and 0.5 minutes is 30 seconds. Using 480 minutes in the division ignores the lost time and gives 33 seconds. The remaining options divide by the wrong demand or pair the wrong availability figure with the wrong demand.
  21. The correct answer is C (The mean sits high and both tails cross a limit, so spread and centering both matter): The histogram shows a cluster of readings centered a little above the nominal target, with two readings below the lower specification limit and three readings above the upper limit. Two readings plus three readings is five out of forty, so the process loses about 12.5 percent of its output, and the loss comes from both the position of the mean and the spread. The tallest bar sits inside the limits, but that does not make the process capable. The distribution is not skewed low, and shifting the mean alone would not remove the low tail.
  22. The correct answer is A (Write an operational definition of a defect and retrain the inspectors on it): An operational definition states in plain terms what counts as a defect and how each one is judged, so two people applying the same rule reach the same answer. Training then confirms that both inspectors apply it the same way. Replacing an inspector or enlarging the sample changes the measurement process without fixing the rule. Averaging two different judgments produces a number that describes neither inspector.
  23. The correct answer is B (Reproducibility is poor, so operator technique and training should be examined first): When one operator repeats the same reading but two operators differ on the same parts, the variation lies between operators, which is reproducibility, and it points to technique, training, or how each person holds and reads the gauge. Repeatability would show up as scatter within a single operator's repeated readings. Bias and linearity concern the gauge against a reference value or across the measuring range, which the study does not describe.
  24. The correct answer is D (The mean is 16, the median is 14, and the high value pulls the mean upward): The five readings add to 80, so 80 divided by 5 gives a mean of 16, while the middle value in order is 14, so the median is 14. The reading of 24 sits well above the rest and pulls the mean upward, which is why the mean and the median differ. The data set is not symmetric because of that reading. The mode is 14 rather than 16, and the range is 24 less 12, which is 12.
  25. The correct answer is D (The Poisson distribution, because it models counts of independent events in an interval): The Poisson distribution models the number of independent events that occur in a fixed interval when the average rate is stable, which matches calls arriving per minute. The binomial distribution needs a fixed number of trials with two outcomes for each one. The normal distribution models a continuous measurement rather than a count. The uniform distribution describes values spread evenly across a range, which does not match arrivals that vary from minute to minute.
  26. The correct answer is B (Cpk is 0.89, which shows the process is not centered between the limits): Cp is the tolerance width divided by six standard deviations, so 506 minus 494 is 12, and 12 divided by 9 gives 1.33. Cpk uses the nearer specification, so the upper side is 506 minus 502, which is 4, and 4 divided by 4.5 is 0.89, while the lower side is 502 minus 494, which is 8, and 8 divided by 4.5 is 1.78. The smaller figure governs, so Cpk is 0.89 and the process is not centered. The option giving 1.33 reports Cp, and the option giving 1.78 reports the lower side alone.
  27. The correct answer is C (It is expected defects per million opportunities when the process runs at four sigma): Defects per million opportunities is the count of defects expected for every million chances to make one, so a figure of 6,210 means about 6,210 defects per million opportunities. Sigma levels are converted with the common 1.5 sigma shift convention, which is why four sigma maps to about 6,210 and six sigma maps to about 3.4. The figure is not a pass rate after rework, not a count of inspected units, and not a revenue based complaint rate.
  28. The correct answer is C (The two variables move together in a linear pattern, so a speed change is worth testing): A correlation of negative 0.82 shows a strong linear association in which higher line speed goes with lower fill weight, and that is a sound reason to run a controlled test of the speed change. Correlation does not establish which variable causes the other, so the slower line cannot be promised as a fix. The direction of influence is not settled by the plot, and one observational study does not close the question.
  29. The correct answer is C (The difference is unlikely under the null hypothesis, so the team can reject it now): A p value of 0.03 is the chance of seeing a difference this large when the null hypothesis is true, and 0.03 sits below the alpha of 0.05, so the null hypothesis is rejected. The p value is not the chance that the new form is better, and a smaller p value is evidence against the null rather than a reason to keep it. The result does speak to the decision, so waiting for a second study is a choice about risk rather than a requirement of the test.
  30. The correct answer is D (A shift related cause is likely, so the team should stratify the data by shift): When variation between shifts is larger than variation within a shift, the shift itself is a likely source, so stratifying by shift is the step that separates the groups and shows where the cause sits. Treating the variation as random would leave that source hidden. The measurement system is not indicated here, because the study compares shifts rather than operators or gauges. A stable process would show variation spread evenly rather than concentrated between shifts.
  31. The correct answer is A (A downward linear trend, so weight tends to fall as line speed increases): The plotted points fall in a steady line as speed increases, and the fitted trend line slopes downward, so the diagram shows a negative linear relationship. The scatter is not a random cloud because the points follow a clear direction. The pattern is a straight line rather than a curve, so the option describing a rise and then a leveling off does not match. The slope runs downward rather than upward.
  32. The correct answer is B (A two sample t test comparing the two shift means with a pooled variance): Two independent groups, continuous data, thirty observations per group, and an assumption of equal variances match a two sample t test with a pooled estimate of the common variance. A paired test requires matched observations, which independent shifts do not provide. A chi square test works on counts rather than continuous measurements. Analysis of variance would extend to three or more groups, and this question compares two.
  33. The correct answer is D (The risk priority number is 160, the product of the three rating scales): The risk priority number is severity times occurrence times detection, so 8 times 5 is 40, and 40 times 4 is 160. That single number ranks the failure mode against the others in the register so the team can see where to work first. Adding the three ratings gives 17, which is not how the scales combine. Using only severity and occurrence gives 40, and using only occurrence and detection gives 20, and neither of those covers all three scales.
  34. The correct answer is C (Work on scratches, because the top two bars cover about two thirds of defects): Scratches account for 42 defects and dents for 25, so the two largest bars cover 42 plus 25, which is 67 of the 100 defects counted, or about two thirds of the total. That is the concentration a Pareto chart is built to reveal, so the first funded project belongs on the largest bar. The remaining defect types are smaller individually, and spreading the funding across all five dilutes the effort instead of concentrating it.
  35. The correct answer is A (Stratify the stops by shift, operator, and material lot to look for a pattern): The log already records the shift, the operator, the settings, and the material lot, so splitting the four events by those factors is the step that may reveal a shared condition behind the stops. Recalling events from memory adds a weaker record to data the team already holds. Replacing the controller acts on a guess before any pattern is tested. A designed experiment comes later, after the likely cause has been narrowed.
  36. The correct answer is B (Running the same combination of settings more than once to estimate error): Replication is a repeat of the same treatment combination, and it gives the experiment an estimate of pure error so the observed effects can be judged against normal variation. Randomizing the run order and testing each factor at two levels are parts of the design, but neither one creates the repeat. Testing three factors at once describes the size of the experiment rather than the repeat itself.
  37. The correct answer is D (Eight runs, which is two raised to the power of three): A full factorial with three factors at two levels has two multiplied by two multiplied by two combinations, which is eight runs. Each combination is tested once, so the design needs eight runs. Three runs would test only one setting per factor and leave the interactions unseen. Six runs do not form a complete set of combinations, and multiplying the factor count by the level count gives nine, which is not how a two level factorial is sized.
  38. The correct answer is A (Cut the signature wait by moving approvals online and removing the two day queue): Waiting is the largest of the three wastes described, and the file sits for two days before anyone touches it, so removing that queue returns the most lead time for the effort involved. Moving files between buildings and re-entering data are smaller wastes that can be tackled afterward. Hiring a clerk or extending the approval window adds cost or time rather than removing the delay.
  39. The correct answer is A (About 12 minutes of value added time out of a 35 minute lead time): The value added times are 2, 3, 5, and 2 minutes, which add to 12 minutes, and the non value added times are 6, 12, 3, and 2 minutes, which add to 23 minutes. The total lead time is 12 plus 23, which is 35 minutes, so the customer would pay for about 12 minutes out of the 35. The second option swaps the two totals, and the last option adds the lead time to itself.
  40. The correct answer is D (Five S, which organizes a workspace so every item has a set place): Labeled bins, a fixed order for supplies, and a daily check are the set in order, standardize, and sustain practices of Five S, which give every item a known place and a routine for keeping it there. Kanban signals the need for more material rather than organizing a station. Poka yoke prevents a wrong step at the point of use. Kaizen is the improvement activity itself rather than the arrangement of the workspace.
  41. The correct answer is B (Pilot the change at one plant, measure the result, then standardize and roll it out): A pilot proves the change in a second setting, and measuring it before the wider rollout keeps the team from spreading a method that only worked on the original line. Standardizing the work then gives the remaining plants a defined method to adopt. Sending a document asks each plant to interpret the change on its own, and rolling out everywhere at once leaves no room to correct a problem. Letting each plant adapt freely makes the results hard to compare.
  42. The correct answer is A (About 66.5 percent, the product of availability, performance, and quality): Availability is 480 minus 60, which is 420, divided by 480, which is 0.875. Performance is 0.80 and quality is 0.95, so the product is 0.875 times 0.80 times 0.95, which is 0.665, or about 66.5 percent. The option giving 70 percent stops after availability and performance, the option giving 83 percent stops after availability and quality, and the option giving 87.5 percent averages the three rates instead of multiplying them.
  43. The correct answer is A (A special cause is likely present, so the process should be investigated at that time): A point above the upper control limit is a signal of a special cause, and the operator has already ruled out a specification change, so the process should be examined for what was different when subgroup seven was produced. Control limits come from the process variation rather than from the specifications, so a point can signal while still sitting inside the customer limits. Recalculating the limits or waiting for a second point both delay the investigation the signal calls for.
  44. The correct answer is C (An np chart, because it plots the count of nonconforming units with a constant sample size): Each invoice is judged as conforming or nonconforming, so the count in each batch is a count of nonconforming units, and the batch size stays at 200, which is the constant sample size the np chart needs. A p chart suits a sample size that changes from one period to the next. A c chart counts defects rather than defective units, and an X bar and R chart needs a continuous measurement on each item.
  45. The correct answer is D (Tightening limits reacts to common cause variation and would raise variation over time): Points that bounce randomly inside the limits come from common cause variation, which belongs to the design of the process, and reacting to it with tighter limits is tampering that tends to add variation rather than remove it. Control limits are computed from the process data and are not adjusted to make the chart more sensitive. Setting the limits at the specification limits confuses control limits with customer limits. Only a real change in the process justifies new limits, and the run described shows no such change.
  46. The correct answer is B (Visual controls and standard work that make the correct method the normal one): A posted work sheet, marked floor positions, and a short daily check are visual controls and standard work, and together they make the correct method the easy one to follow and to notice when it drifts. A yearly audit checks the process after the fact. A control plan is the document that names the response to a signal, and a measurement study checks the scales. The activity described is the daily visual system rather than any of those three.
  47. The correct answer is D (The reaction plan that states the response when a chart signals an out of control point): A control plan tells the operator what to plot and when, and the reaction plan tells them what to do when a signal appears, so without it the chart produces a signal with no defined response. The owner names and the sampling details are already in the plan. Team member lists, test summaries, and storyboards are project records rather than instructions for running the process after handover.
  48. The correct answer is A (Assign a process owner, audit the measures, and review the control charts on a schedule): A sustained gain needs someone who owns the process after the project closes, along with regular checks that the measures are still collected and the charts are still reviewed. That routine is what keeps the improvement from fading when the project team moves on. Reforming the team puts the monitoring back on people who no longer own the work. Retraining employees on project tools or raising the goal does not put a check in place.
  49. The correct answer is C (DMADV, because it defines, measures, analyzes, designs, and then verifies each new process): A new process with requirements that are not yet set follows the design for Six Sigma path, and DMADV carries the work through define, measure, analyze, design, and verify so the design is proven before launch. DMAIC improves a process that already exists, and PDCA is a general improvement cycle. DMADOV is also a design path, but it is not DMAIC with an added optimize step, so that description of the roadmap is wrong.
  50. The correct answer is B (Statistical tolerancing, which combines the tolerance distributions to set realistic design limits): Statistical tolerancing treats each part dimension as a distribution rather than a fixed worst case value, and combining those distributions shows the assembly variation that is likely, which allows wider individual tolerances while the assembly still fits. Worst case tolerance design stacks every part at its limit, which is a conservative method that usually forces tighter and costlier tolerances. Design for test and design for maintainability pursue different design goals and do not set the tolerance stack.
  51. The correct answer is C (Improvement is project based, with a pipeline that runs beside the business, not through it): The team has trained belts and regular tollgate reviews, which rules out the ad hoc stage, but the projects sit outside the operating plan. That places the organization at the project based stage, where a pipeline exists and the work runs beside the business. The systematic stage requires projects to trace to strategic goals with finance confirming the savings, and the continuous improvement stage requires leaders to coach and suppliers to take part. Options A and B describe those other stages.
  52. The correct answer is A (Run a DMAIC project that measures the process before testing possible causes): An unknown cause that repeats and lacks baseline data is the classic case for DMAIC, because the team must measure before it can separate real causes from opinions. A kaizen event fits visible waste that a team can remove in days, and the theory of constraints fits a single bottleneck that limits the whole system. DMADV fits a process that does not exist yet, which is not the case here.
  53. The correct answer is D (Review the weights and scores, since a modest weight change can flip the ranking): The weighted totals already put Project X first at 425 against 410, but closing the choice without checking the weights leaves the member's point unanswered. The note that Project Y leads on customer impact and feasibility matters because those criteria carry real weight, and a small change in the weights or the scores can reverse the order. Starting both projects spreads the same staff across two efforts, which slows both.
  54. The correct answer is B (Assign process owners, document the new standards, and review measures on a fixed cadence): Sustained gains depend on clear ownership, written standards, and a regular review rhythm that survives a change in leadership. Posting savings on a wall is communication, not control, and it does not tell anyone what to do when a measure slips. Asking people to work on improvement tasks during personal time and deferring reviews both weaken the new process rather than protect it.
  55. The correct answer is B (Value added time is 5 minutes and efficiency is 25 percent of lead time): The two value added steps take 2 plus 3, which equals 5 minutes, and the non value added steps take 6 plus 9, which equals 15 minutes. Total lead time is 5 plus 15, which equals 20 minutes, so process cycle efficiency is 5 divided by 20, which equals 0.25 or 25 percent. Option A reverses the two totals, option C treats every step as value added, and option D misstates the efficiency as 75 percent.
  56. The correct answer is D (About 79 percent, the probability a unit passes every step without rework): Rolled throughput yield multiplies the first pass yields of the steps, so 0.95 times 0.90 times 0.98 times 0.94 equals 0.855 times 0.98 times 0.94, which equals 0.8379 times 0.94, or 0.7876. That is about 79 percent, so the process passes roughly four units in five without a single rework loop. Averaging, taking the median, or taking the highest step yield all hide the compounding effect of several steps.
  57. The correct answer is A (A Pareto chart that ranks complaint categories by count and shows cumulative share): A Pareto chart sorts categories from largest to smallest and adds a cumulative line, so leaders can see how much of the total volume the top few categories explain. A control chart tracks a measure over time against control limits, a run chart shows a trend or a shift, and a histogram shows the shape of a single distribution. None of those three answers the question about category share.
  58. The correct answer is C (Compare cycle time per application, since the branches handle similar volumes): Cycle time per application normalizes the measure, so the comparison is fair even when the two branches have slightly different totals. Comparing total time, the busiest week, or the single longest case mixes volume and scale into the result. Per unit measures let the team compare like with like before drawing a conclusion.
  59. The correct answer is A (Use a structured method such as nominal group technique to collect ideas first): A structured method asks each member to write ideas first and then shares them in turn, which reduces the chance that two voices control the room. Writing before speaking gives quiet members time to form a view, and it stops the first strong opinion from anchoring the discussion. Asking the dominant members to speak last is a small tweak that does not change the underlying pattern, and cutting the team or handing decisions to the sponsor removes the input the team was formed to provide.
  60. The correct answer is D (Creating a team charter that names the sponsor, roles, and decision rights): A charter records the problem, goal, scope, roles, and the decisions each role can make, so the team knows who approves a change and who reports progress. An agenda, a newsletter, and a room booking are logistics, and none of them assigns authority. Clear decision rights shorten debates because people know where a question belongs.
  61. The correct answer is C (Coach the team, then shift decisions to them as their skill grows): Situational leaders raise the team capability in steps, giving more authority as members show they can handle it. Continuing to direct every task keeps the team dependent, while leaving the project entirely removes the support the team still needs. Asking for a new leader skips the development step and treats the problem as a person swap.
  62. The correct answer is B (Agree on a data collection plan that can test both explanations fairly): When two people offer competing explanations, a short plan that defines the measure and the sample lets the data settle the question without making it personal. The plan also states how many readings are enough, which prevents either side from stopping the study early. Backing the experienced member rewards seniority instead of evidence, and asking the sponsor or taking a vote picks a winner without learning which explanation is true.
  63. The correct answer is D (Leadership has not freed time or shown active sponsorship for the team): Missed tollgate dates plus absent members plus a sponsor who has not attended for six weeks point to a leadership and time problem, which no tool or scope change will fix. A sponsor who stays away cannot remove barriers, and members who cannot leave daily work have no protected time for project tasks. Software training, a schedule written before the charter, and a narrow scope are common project issues, but the evidence in this situation points to the support role.
  64. The correct answer is A (A project charter that defines the problem, goal, scope, and team): The charter is the founding document that states the problem, the measurable goal, the boundaries, and the team, so it settles the scope argument before work begins. A control plan comes after the improvement, a cause and effect diagram explores causes, and a failure mode list ranks risks. Each of those has a place later in the project.
  65. The correct answer is C (A quality function deployment matrix that maps needs to requirements): Quality function deployment, often called the house of quality, translates customer wants into measurable design requirements and shows how strongly each requirement serves each want. The matrix also forces the team to state targets for each requirement, so engineers can test the design against the stated wants. A control chart, a Pareto chart, and a process map each describe or monitor existing data rather than connect wants to requirements.
  66. The correct answer is B (A defect is any application with at least one missing required document): An operational definition states exactly what is counted, in terms the collector can apply the same way every time, so a missing required document is a concrete and repeatable rule. It also lets the team count opportunities, which is the number of chances a defect could occur, so a defect rate can be computed. Letting a manager decide case by case, counting complaints, or using a vague expectation all produce counts that drift between people and days.
  67. The correct answer is A (Reduce coating thickness variation by 30 percent within four months): A good goal states a measure, a direction, a target, and a deadline, so progress can be judged at any point. The other statements describe a wish, a study, or an open ended fix, and none of them can be checked against a baseline. A measurable goal also tells the team when the project is finished.
  68. The correct answer is D (Define the population and measure the whole process, not a convenient slice): Excluding short calls changes the population, so the average no longer describes the process the customer experiences. The team should define the population first and then measure it as a whole. The other options reduce effort, trim outliers, or narrow the sample, and each one trades away the validity of the result.
  69. The correct answer is C (Walk the process together and build one agreed current state map): People from different areas often describe the same process in different ways, so the team must walk it together and settle on one shared picture before it can find the delays. A future state map, an experiment, or a set of separate maps all come after the current state is agreed. The shared walk also builds ownership of the findings.
  70. The correct answer is B (Identify each customer group and how the process delivers value to it): A process can serve several customer groups, and the charter becomes clearer when the team names each group and the value the process owes it. The team can then translate each need into a requirement and a measure, which keeps the project focused on what customers actually receive. Choosing the biggest payer, serving only internal groups, or ranking by ease of access all leave out a customer whose needs still have to be met.
  71. The correct answer is A (A special cause is present, so the team should investigate subgroup 7): On the chart, subgroup 7 sits at 13.4 while the upper control limit is 13.0, so the point falls outside the limit and signals a special cause. Control limits come from the process data and describe what the process normally does, so a point beyond them calls for a search for the cause. Widening the specification limits or dismissing the point would hide the signal rather than explain it.
  72. The correct answer is C (A gauge repeatability and reproducibility study of the measurement system): A gauge R and R study has several appraisers measure the same parts more than once, which separates the variation from the measurement system from the variation between the parts. Repeatability is the variation when one appraiser measures the same part again, and reproducibility is the variation between appraisers. A capability study, a control chart, and a tolerance study answer different questions.
  73. The correct answer is B (Median and interquartile range, because they resist the pull of outliers): Skewed data with a few very long waits pull the mean upward, so the mean and standard deviation overstate the typical case. The median marks the middle value, and the interquartile range covers the middle half of the data, so both stay steady when a few extreme values appear. The mode and the range carry less information, and the mean with the range mixes a sensitive measure with an unstable one.
  74. The correct answer is D (A small share of units falls below the lower limit, so the process produces defects): The lowest bin runs from 95 to 97 grams and holds two units, and the lower specification limit is 97 grams, so those two units sit below the limit. The bulk of the distribution sits between 99 and 103 grams, which shows the process is mostly inside the limits but still ships defects. The chart does not show a left skew, a spread beyond both limits, or a perfectly centered process.
  75. The correct answer is A (Cp is 1.00 and Cpk is 1.00, since the process is centered): Cp equals the width between the limits divided by six standard deviations, so 10.30 minus 9.70 equals 0.60, and 6 times 0.10 equals 0.60, giving 1.00. Cpk takes the smaller distance from the mean to a limit, divided by three standard deviations, so 0.30 divided by 0.30 equals 1.00 on both sides. Because the mean sits at the center, Cp and Cpk are equal, and option C also reverses the meaning of the two indices.
  76. The correct answer is B (With the shift, 3.4 defects per million corresponds to about 6 sigma): The 1.5 sigma shift convention is what links a long term defect rate of 3.4 per million to a reported level of about six sigma, and it accounts for the drift a process shows over time. Without the shift, six sigma would mean far fewer than 3.4 defects per million, so option D is not right. The unshifted long term value for 3.4 defects per million is 4.5 sigma, so the other options attach the wrong sigma level or the wrong direction to the convention.
  77. The correct answer is C (The night shift holds longer and varies more, with a higher median and wider spread): The night group has a median of 14 minutes against 8 minutes for the day group, so its typical hold is longer. Its range runs from 6 to 20 minutes while the day group runs from 4 to 13, so the night group also varies more. The other options reverse the medians or claim equal spread.
  78. The correct answer is D (The system is repeatable but biased, since every reading sits above the true value): Repeatability is the closeness of repeated readings to each other, and the ten readings are close, so the system is repeatable. Accuracy is closeness to the true value, and every reading sits five units high, so the system carries a bias that can be corrected by calibration. Options A and B swap the two properties.
  79. The correct answer is D (They keep different collectors counting the same event the same way): An operational definition states exactly what counts as a defect or an opportunity, so two people collecting data reach the same answer. Without it, counts drift between collectors and the team compares numbers that mean different things. Fewer observations, less training, and flexible measures all trade away data quality.
  80. The correct answer is C (Correlation supports association, and a cause needs more evidence than one study): A correlation of negative 0.85 shows a strong downward association, so as speed rises, fill weight tends to fall. Association alone does not show which variable drives the other, and some third factor could move both. The remaining options misread the strength or the sign of the correlation.
  81. The correct answer is D (Scratches, because it is the largest bar and gives the largest first gain): Scratches has 42 of the 90 total defects, which is 42 divided by 90, or about 47 percent of the total. Attacking the largest category usually removes the most defects for the effort spent, and the team can recheck the Pareto after the fix. The other options pick a smaller category or a mixed group.
  82. The correct answer is B (A two sample t test, because the groups are independent and means are compared): Two separate sets of boards give independent samples, and the question asks whether the two means differ, so a two sample t test fits. Similar variances and roughly normal data also support this test, which compares the difference in means against the variation within the groups. A paired t test needs the same units measured twice, a chi square test compares counts in categories, and an F test for variances compares spread rather than center.
  83. The correct answer is C (A curved residual pattern points to a missing curve or interaction term): A residual plot should show a random cloud around zero, so a curved pattern means the straight line model leaves a real effect unexplained. The team should add a curve term or an interaction and refit the model. A high R squared does not rescue a model whose residuals carry a pattern, so option A is not a safe conclusion.
  84. The correct answer is D (Act on the high severity mode, since a serious effect needs attention): Severity 8 means a serious effect on the customer or the process, and the risk priority number is 8 times 4 times 6, which equals 192. A high severity mode deserves action even when the total score sits below a local threshold, and the team should reduce severity, occurrence, or detection depending on what the design allows. Detection is often the least effective lever on its own.
  85. The correct answer is B (Gather data on the shortlisted causes to see which ones matter): A cause and effect diagram organizes possible causes, but it does not rank them, so the team narrows the list and then collects data to see which causes actually move the result. A well chosen check of a few causes can confirm or remove each one, and the team keeps the causes that survive the test. Voting, counting ideas per branch, or redrawing the picture all produce a ranking from opinion rather than evidence.
  86. The correct answer is A (The combined defect rate is about 8.8 percent, not the simple sum of the steps): If the two steps are independent, the chance a unit passes both is 0.96 times 0.95, which equals 0.912, so the chance it fails at least one step is 1 minus 0.912, which equals 0.088, or 8.8 percent. Adding the rates overstates the total because some units would fail both steps. Multiplying alone or dividing the rates both give the wrong figure.
  87. The correct answer is C (It estimates each main effect and the interaction between the two factors): A full factorial runs every combination of the factor levels, so with two factors at two levels it needs four runs and can estimate both main effects and their interaction. A design with fewer runs may alias the interaction with a main effect. Averaging the levels does not remove noise, and normality of the response is an assumption rather than a result of the design.
  88. The correct answer is A (Temperature has the larger effect, and the two factors show no interaction): The temperature effect of 6.5 is much larger than the pressure effect of 1.2, so temperature drives the response. The interaction is the change in the temperature effect as pressure moves from low to high, and its value here is 6.5 minus 6.5, which equals zero, so the lines run parallel. Options that give pressure the larger effect or claim an interaction misread the table.
  89. The correct answer is B (Motion, because the walking path adds distance without adding value): Walking 120 meters to reach a rack 20 meters away is movement that the customer does not pay for, so the waste is motion. Transportation covers moving product rather than people, so it does not describe pickers walking the route. Inventory, waiting, and overprocessing are other wastes, but the evidence in this situation points to distance and movement. Removing the travel first shortens the lead time before any picking rule changes.
  90. The correct answer is D (About 20 containers, since the half day buffer holds 240 prescriptions): Daily demand is 480 prescriptions, and a half day buffer holds 480 times 0.5, which equals 240 prescriptions. Each container holds 12 prescriptions, so 240 divided by 12 equals 20 containers. Ten, twenty four, and forty containers all come from using the wrong buffer or the wrong divisor.
  91. The correct answer is C (About 73.5 percent, after availability, performance, and quality are multiplied): Availability is 420 divided by 480, which equals 0.875. Performance is 0.42 times 900 divided by 420, which equals 378 divided by 420, or 0.90, and quality is 840 good parts divided by 900, or 0.9333. Multiplying 0.875 by 0.90 by 0.9333 gives 0.735, or about 73.5 percent, so the other options just quote a single factor.
  92. The correct answer is A (Single minute exchange of die, which converts internal tasks to external ones): Single minute exchange of die, usually shortened to SMED, studies a changeover, separates tasks that must happen while the machine is stopped from tasks that can happen while it runs, and moves as many as possible to the outside. That is exactly what filming, sorting, and moving tasks offline describe. Total productive maintenance, control charting, and failure mode analysis serve other purposes.
  93. The correct answer is D (Exploit the press first, so no capacity is lost to breakdowns or idle time): The theory of constraints says to identify the constraint, exploit it, subordinate other decisions to it, and only then elevate it with more capacity. Exploiting the press means removing breakdowns, idle time, and bad parts at the bottleneck before spending on a second machine or a night shift. Buying capacity or adding hours before the exploit step leaves easy gains on the table.
  94. The correct answer is B (It is built into the system, so changing limits or tinkering adds variation): Common cause variation comes from the many small sources built into the process, and the process is stable while those sources act. Tightening the limits or adjusting the machine in response to common cause movement is tampering, which adds variation rather than reducing it. Special causes, not common causes, are the ones the chart is designed to flag.
  95. The correct answer is A (An exponentially weighted moving average chart, which weights recent points): An exponentially weighted moving average chart blends each new point with the history of the chart, giving the most recent points the most weight, so it reacts to small shifts faster than a plain chart. A p chart tracks a proportion, a c chart counts defects per sample, and an R chart tracks within subgroup range. Each answers a different question.
  96. The correct answer is C (A p chart, because the measure is a proportion of failing opportunities): The measure is the fraction of opportunities that fail, and a p chart is built for the proportion of defective or nonconforming units in a sample. An X bar and R chart works with measured values rather than proportions, a c chart counts defects in a constant inspection unit, and a u chart counts defects per unit. The p chart fits the reported measure.
  97. The correct answer is D (A control plan that lists the owner, measure, reaction plan, and review cycle): A control plan records what is monitored, who owns it, the limits or target, the action to take when a signal appears, and how often the measure is reviewed. That document keeps the gain alive after the project team steps away. A charter, a data collection plan, and a cause and effect diagram all belong to earlier stages.
  98. The correct answer is B (Reduce common cause variation by changing the process design itself): A stable process with a 2 percent defect rate has common cause variation, so the way to lower the rate is to change the process design rather than tighten limits or inspect more. Narrowing limits flags ordinary points as special, and extra inspection catches defects without preventing them. Recalculating limits with recent data does not change the process.
  99. The correct answer is A (Define, Measure, Analyze, Design, Verify, which fits a new offering): DMADV stands for Define, Measure, Analyze, Design, and Verify, and it fits a product or process that does not exist yet because it designs quality in from the start. DMAIC is the improvement roadmap for an existing process, so option B describes the wrong method. The other two sequences mix phase names from different roadmaps.
  100. The correct answer is C (Worst case adds the tolerances, and statistical tolerancing uses the root sum square): Worst case tolerance stacking adds each part tolerance to the assembly, which assumes every part sits at its own extreme. Statistical tolerancing combines the same tolerances with the root sum square, which reflects the low chance that every part reaches its extreme at once. The two methods do not give the same answer, and statistical tolerancing does not assume every part sits at its limit.
  101. The correct answer is B (The size of the gap and the complexity of its likely causes): Six Sigma pays off when the gap from the goal is large and the cause is not already obvious, because the method spends real time on measurement and analysis. A small problem with a known cause is handled faster with a short improvement exercise. Counting complaints (A), checking the consultant budget (C), and weighing the reporter's seniority (D) describe the politics around a problem rather than whether it needs the full method.
  102. An early stage group runs projects as one-off events, while a mature group ties improvement to strategy and daily management. The number of certified belts (B), the size of the quality budget (C), and the count of finished projects (D) are activity counts, and activity counts can run high while the culture stays unchanged.
  103.   104. That map tells the Black Belt who needs early involvement and who needs a specific response plan. A statistical test (B), a control plan (C), and a pilot study (D) are later technical steps and do not identify allies and blockers.
  105.   106. The study follows a same-market competitor, so it fits competitive benchmarking.
  107. The correct answer is D (Net present value, which discounts the future cash flows): Net present value converts each year's return into today's dollars using the required rate, then subtracts the investment, so it answers whether the project creates value. Return on investment (A) ignores the timing of the cash flows, and payback period (B) ignores the time value of money and any cash flow after the point of payback. Cost benefit analysis (C) can list and weigh benefits, but it does not by itself apply the discount rate the finance team supplied.
  108.   109. Performing (D) is the later stage of smooth execution, which does not match the arguing and frustration described.
  110. The correct answer is D (The nominal group technique, which mixes silent writing with ranking): The nominal group technique has members write ideas alone, records every idea, discusses them as a group, and then ranks or rates them privately to reach a decision. Multi-voting (A) narrows a list with repeated votes rather than silent generation and private ranking. Consensus building (B) and open brainstorming (C) do not follow the silent write, discuss, then rank sequence described.
  111. Coaching (A) also invites discussion and reviews the work together, which the scenario does not describe. Supporting (C) and delegating (D) hand over more of the decision, which the member is not ready for yet.
  112. Digression (A) is a loss of focus, and dominant participation (B) is one member controlling the airtime. Reluctant participation (C) points to members who rarely speak, while here they spoke after the meeting and chose not to speak in it.
  113.   114.   115.   126. The correct answer is C (The addition rule, because the two events cannot occur together): When two events cannot happen at the same time they are mutually exclusive, and the chance of either one is the sum of their separate probabilities. The multiplication rule (A) applies to independent events happening together, and the complement rule (B) is about the chance that an event does not happen. The conditional rule (D) is for the chance of one event given that another has occurred.
  127. The correct answer is A (The process spread is narrow, but the mean sits off center): Cp measures the spread against the specification width, so 1.60 says the process is narrow enough.   129.   130. A type I error (A) is rejecting a true null hypothesis, which is the opposite mistake. The test did not have a power failure of the kind named in option C, and option D confuses the error with a defect in how the samples were drawn.

A negative correlation (B) is not required for a causal link, and dropping training hours (D) would throw away useful information instead of testing the relationship.
