Multiplying them gives 0.90 x 0.90 x 0.95 = 0.7695, which rounds to about 0.77. Averaging the three values (A) is not how OEE is built, and options C and D report only one of the three factors.
  144. The correct answer is A (The limits come from the process data and tampering will add variation): Control limits are calculated from the process itself, so they show how much variation is built into the system. Tightening them because the points look unpleasant is tampering, which adds variation and can make performance worse. Specification limits (C) are set by the customer or design and are not the basis for control limits, and tighter limits (D) would raise false alarms rather than improve detection.
  145. The correct answer is D (A special cause is present and the process should be investigated): Point 7 sits at 13.4, which is above the upper control limit of 13.0, and a point beyond a control limit is a standard signal of a special cause. The other points fall inside the limits in a random pattern, so the process is not simply wandering as a whole. Recalculating the limits (A) would hide the signal, and calling the point common cause (C) ignores the rule the chart is built on.
  146. The correct answer is B (Subgroups show within hour variation and make shifts easier to see): Rational subgrouping collects items close together in time so that variation within the subgroup reflects ordinary short-term noise, while shifts between subgroups stand out. Option A turns that claim around, because the range chart reports the variation inside each hour rather than hiding it. Plotting every part by itself would mix the sources of variation and make the shifts harder to see, not easier. Subgroups do not remove the need for a measurement study (D), and they are not limited to full inspection (C).
  147. The correct answer is A (A p chart, which tracks the proportion of defectives): A p chart works with the proportion of defective units and allows the sample size to change from period to period. An np chart (B) needs a constant sample size, which the hospital does not have. A c chart (C) counts defects in a constant sized unit, and an X-bar and R chart (D) needs measured values, so neither fits a daily count of complete or incomplete kits.
  148. The correct answer is B (A control plan, which lists measures, owners, and responses): A control plan names the measure to watch, the person responsible, the frequency, and the reaction when the measure drifts, which is what sustains the gain after the team leaves. A lessons learned file (A) captures experience, and a gage R and R study (C) checks the measurement system. A charter revision (D) updates project paperwork rather than the running process.
  149. The correct answer is A (DMADV, which designs a new process to meet customer needs): DMADV, which stands for define, measure, analyze, design, and validate, is used when a new process or product must be created to meet customer requirements. DMAIC (B) improves a process that already exists, which is not the case here. PDCA (C) is a general improvement cycle, and kanban (D) is a flow control method rather than a design methodology.
  150. The correct answer is B (The three part variations are independent and rarely peak together): Statistical tolerancing combines independent part variations by the root sum of squares instead of adding the worst case values, because each part is unlikely to sit at its extreme at the same moment. With three equal tolerances, 3t = 0.30 mm gives t = 0.10 mm, and the root sum of squares is 0.10 x the square root of 3 = 0.173 mm, about 0.17 mm. Perfect correlation (A) would justify adding the values, and the supplier contract (D) does not set the statistical assumption.
  151. The correct answer is A (Six Sigma, since a low defect rate needs careful measurement work.): The screening rule is to match the method to the nature of the problem. Lean removes waste and improves flow, which fits delay, travel, and inventory problems. A rate that is stable but still too high is a variation problem, so Six Sigma tools such as capability studies and designed experiments fit the work. Options B, C, and D either describe a flow problem, postpone action, or avoid choosing a method from evidence.
  152. The correct answer is C (Six Sigma began at Motorola, and Welch later promoted the method at GE.): Six Sigma was created at Motorola in 1986, where engineer Bill Smith developed the methodology. Jack Welch championed it at General Electric years later, so the two companies are often mixed up. Options A, B, and D place the origin at the wrong company or reverse the order of events.
  153. The correct answer is B (The site improves separate steps but has not linked projects to results.): A maturity model describes how far an organization has moved from isolated tool use toward strategy driven deployment. Cutting travel distance in local kaizen events while on time delivery stays flat is the usual symptom of projects that are not tied to a business result. Training, software, and event volume are useful signals, but none of them explains the missing outcome.
  154. The correct answer is D (Plan for funding delays, staff losses, and a possible change in lending rules.): SWOT analysis names internal strengths and weaknesses beside external opportunities and threats, and contingency planning then prepares a response for the threats that could disrupt the deployment. Naming the specific risks with their responses is what turns the analysis into a plan. Option A stops inside the listing and names no risk, and options B and C are portfolio and staffing tasks with no risk response.
  155. The correct answer is A (Internal benchmarking, followed by competitive benchmarking against a rival.): Benchmarking types are named by what is compared and against whom. Comparing two units inside one company is internal benchmarking, and comparing with a direct competitor is competitive benchmarking. Process benchmarking compares work practices rather than results, and generic benchmarking compares with organizations outside the industry, so options B and C name the wrong pair, while option D reverses the order.
  156. The correct answer is D (A payback of eight months and a first year return on investment near 50 percent.): Simple payback divides the project cost by the yearly saving, so 60,000 divided by 90,000 equals 0.67 years, which is about eight months. Return on investment divides the net gain by the cost, so 90,000 minus 60,000 equals 30,000, and 30,000 divided by 60,000 equals 0.50, or about 50 percent. Option B keeps the right payback but divides the saving by the cost, which gives the benefit cost ratio of 150 percent, and options A and C use an inverted payback.
  157. The correct answer is D (A leading measure such as the share of calls with the account confirmed weekly.): Leading measures move before the outcome, so the team can act while the result is still forming. Confirming the account before advising is tracked every week and helps drive later satisfaction, which makes it a leading indicator. Option A is the lagging outcome itself, and options B and C are operational counts that do not predict satisfaction as directly.
  158. The correct answer is C (A stakeholder analysis of inputs, interests, and the process owner.): Stakeholder analysis lists the parties who supply input, the parties affected by the output, and the process owner who is accountable for results. That view exposes where handoffs create delay and who must agree before the process can change. Options A, B, and D are capability, risk, and cost tools that answer different questions.
  159. The correct answer is C (The champion, who removes barriers and secures the resources needed.): A champion is a senior leader who clears obstacles and supplies resources so the project can proceed. In the scenario the nurse manager owns the process after handover and the analyst works as a team member. A facilitator guides the team's process rather than funding the work or removing barriers.
  160. The correct answer is B (Storming, where conflict about roles and methods tends to surface.): In the storming stage members test ideas and compete for influence, so open disagreement about data or method is expected. A Black Belt can use that conflict by setting ground rules and returning the group to facts. Forming is early politeness, norming is agreement on working rules, and adjourning is disbanding after the work ends.
  161. The correct answer is A (A virtual cross functional team with a named leader and set meeting times.): Members sit in three plants and work different shifts, so the team needs a virtual design, and the sponsor wants one accountable leader, which points to a virtual cross functional team with named leadership. A self directed team sets its own direction, a volunteer virtual team has no clear accountability, and a one week kaizen event cannot deliver a four month redesign.
  162. The correct answer is D (Nominal group technique, where ideas are written first and then ranked.): The nominal group technique asks members to record ideas privately before any discussion, which keeps quiet members from being overshadowed. The list is then clarified and ranked, often with a weighted vote. Brainstorming is open and verbal, multivoting trims an existing list, and consensus discusses until agreement forms without the written first step.
  163. The correct answer is C (A needs assessment, which matches required skills against the skills present.): Selection criteria and training plans both flow from a needs assessment that compares the skills the project requires with the skills the candidates already hold. The gap tells the Black Belt whom to recruit and what training to deliver before data collection starts. Budget, sponsor preference, and the meeting schedule are constraints rather than the guide for skill fit.
  164. The correct answer is A (The borrower and the regulator are external, while the officer is internal.): External customers receive the product or service, so the borrower and the regulator sit outside the bank. Internal customers work inside the organization and depend on earlier steps, which describes the loan officer and the underwriter. Option B reverses the two groups, option C mislabels the employees, and option D treats the regulator as internal because of the compliance relationship.
  165. The correct answer is D (Observation followed by a focus group with open ended questions.): Watching the work shows what guests actually do, which avoids the bias that comes from asking people to recall and describe their own behavior. A focus group then explores the reasons behind what the observation revealed. Surveys and complaint records are useful methods, but the scenario describes direct watching and a guided group discussion.
  166. The correct answer is C (A lighter frame and the seat are performance needs, while the light is a delighter.): Kano groups requirements by how satisfaction responds as more of the feature is delivered. Stated wants such as a lighter frame and a comfortable seat are performance needs, where better delivery raises satisfaction. A feature that pleases buyers who would not miss it is a delighter, and basic needs are unspoken expectations whose absence would cause strong dissatisfaction.
  167. The correct answer is B (Cut invoice errors from 8 percent to 2 percent by December in billing.): A usable problem statement names the metric, the current level, the target, the process boundary, and the date, all of which this version carries. Options A and C leave the target vague or set no boundary, and option D turns the project into a study that only recommends action.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Balance metrics that track accuracy, rework, and staff workload.): A measurement plan pairs the primary metric with balance measures that reveal whether the improvement moved the problem somewhere else. Accuracy, rework, and workload are typical balance measures for a cycle time project. A larger sample, a control chart, and a rewritten charter address sampling, monitoring, and wording rather than the risk of unintended effects.
  2. The correct answer is D (An activity network diagram that shows dependencies and the critical path.): A network diagram links each activity to the ones that must finish before it can start, which exposes the dependency chain. The longest chain through the network is the critical path, so a delay on that path pushes the project finish. A Gantt chart shows timing, a work breakdown structure divides scope, and a RACI matrix assigns roles.
  3. The correct answer is C (The process runs high, so the upper specification tail is the concern.): The histogram is a mound whose center sits above the 100 gram target and close to the 103 gram upper limit, and part of the output lies beyond that limit. A shift toward one specification limit lowers Cpk even when the spread stays inside the tolerance band. Option A misreads the shape, option B ignores the packages past the limit, and option D calls the spread narrow even though the histogram shows readings beyond both limits.
  4. The correct answer is B (Reproducibility, because the difference lies between the two operators.): In a gauge study, repeatability is the variation seen when one operator measures the same part again with the same gauge, and that variation is small here. Reproducibility is the variation between operators, and an average difference of 0.4 millimeter points to that component. Stability concerns drift over time and linearity concerns accuracy across the measurement range, neither of which the scenario describes.
  5. The correct answer is A (The night shift median is higher and its middle half of times is wider.): On a box plot the line inside the box marks the median and the box spans the first and third quartiles, so a wider night box means more spread in the middle half of the data. The day shift result above the upper fence is an outlier, which is a signal to investigate that case. Option B misreads the medians, option C reverses the shifts, and option D turns one outlier into a claim about the whole process.
  6. The correct answer is D (Stratified sampling, with the population divided and each part sampled.): Stratified sampling divides the population into groups that differ in an important way and then samples inside every group, which preserves the proportions of each category. Simple random sampling draws from one combined list, systematic sampling takes every nth item, and cluster sampling selects whole groups, which is the opposite of sampling inside each group.
  7. The correct answer is C (Calls per day is discrete numeric, and the machine is categorical.): A count of calls can take only whole values, so it is discrete numeric data, and the machine name identifies a category rather than a quantity. Counts are often tracked with run charts or c charts, while machine identity is compared with bar charts or Pareto charts. Option A reverses the two types, and options B and D name scales that do not match the variables.
  8. The correct answer is A (0.2 units, found by dividing the total difference of 1.0 by five.): The differences in the table are 0.2, 0.1, 0.4, 0.0, and 0.3, which add to 1.0. Dividing 1.0 by the five parts gives an average difference of 0.2 units. Option C halves the total, and options B and D divide totals of 2.5 and 2.0 that the table does not support.
  9. The correct answer is B (Cpk is 1.00, and the process is centered between the two limits.): The distance from the mean to the upper limit is 56 minus 50, which equals 6. The distance to the lower limit is 50 minus 44, which also equals 6. Three standard deviations equal 3 times 2, or 6, so Cpk is the smaller of 6 divided by 6 and 6 divided by 6, which is 1.00. The equal distances show a centered process.
  10. The correct answer is D (About 2.3 percent, since the limit is two standard deviations below the mean.): The z value is 492 minus 500, divided by 4, which equals minus 2. About 95.4 percent of a normal distribution lies within two standard deviations of the mean, so about 4.6 percent lies in the two tails and about 2.3 percent lies below the lower limit. Options A, B, and C assign the limit to the wrong number of standard deviations.
  11. The correct answer is C (The first two categories hold about 68 percent, so start with those.): A Pareto chart ranks categories by frequency, and the cumulative line shows how much of the total the leading categories carry. Solder bridges and missing paste account for 96 plus 72, or 168, of the 246 defects, which is about 68 percent. Option A reverses the ranking, option B ignores the concentration, and option D misstates the first bar, which holds about 39 percent.
  12. The correct answer is B (Methods, because unclear requirements describe how the work is defined.): On a cause and effect diagram the methods branch holds the rules, procedures, and definitions that govern the work. Unclear requirements are a defect in how the work is specified, so they belong there. The people branch covers skill and staffing, the measurement branch covers how data is gathered, and the environment branch covers physical or market conditions.
  13. The correct answer is B (The listed causes are candidates, so confirm them with data before acting.): A cause and effect diagram organizes possible causes, and every branch holds only candidates until evidence is collected. Confirming the listed causes with data is the next step, because holding the most notes does not prove that those causes are real. Option A treats an empty branch as completion, option C discards a branch for the wrong reason, and option D turns a list of candidates into proof.
  14. The correct answer is D (A type II error, because the test missed a real difference.): A type I error means rejecting a true null hypothesis, and a type II error means failing to reject a false null hypothesis. The new seal truly works, so the null hypothesis is false and the test missed it, which is a type II error. Options A and B describe the opposite mistake, and option C names power, which is the chance of detecting a real effect.
  15. The correct answer is C (Practical significance, because the size of the gain may not matter.): Statistical significance says an effect is unlikely to be zero, while practical significance asks whether the size of the effect matters to the business. A gain of 0.4 minutes with a small p value can be real and still too small to change what customers experience. Options A, B, and D address sampling, the alpha level, and the wording of hypotheses rather than the size of the gain.
  16. The correct answer is B (The prediction extrapolates beyond the observed range and may not hold there.): A regression equation describes the relationship inside the range of the data used to fit it. A route twice as long as any observed route sits outside that range, and the relationship may change there. Option A misreads R squared, which measures fit inside the data, option C turns an association into cause, and option D suggests an action rather than the reason the prediction is risky.
  17. The correct answer is D (The single test controls the error rate that repeated tests inflate.): Analysis of variance tests all four means at once and holds the overall chance of a false alarm near the chosen alpha. Running six separate two sample tests compounds that chance, so the family wise error rate grows. Option A confuses fewer tests with less data, option B describes a result the analysis does not produce, and option C confuses analysis of variance with a test of medians.
  18. The correct answer is A (Address the severity 9 mode first, because high severity signals serious harm.): The risk priority number is severity times occurrence times detection, so this mode scores 9 times 4 times 3, which equals 108. Severity measures the harm if the failure reaches the customer, so a severity of 9 deserves attention even when another mode has a larger product. Option B treats all three factors as equal, option C invents a fixed threshold, and option D delays work on a serious risk.
  19. The correct answer is C (Two factors, two responses, and six treatment combinations to run.): A factor is a variable the team changes, so temperature and rest time are the two factors, and their settings are levels. Bake time and texture score are the two responses being measured. A two by three full factorial holds 2 times 3, or six, treatment combinations, which the other options misstate.
  20. The correct answer is B (Non value added, because the step uses time without adding customer value.): A step is value added only when it changes the product or service in a way the customer cares about and is done right the first time. Re entering data that already exists uses eleven minutes and does not change what the borrower receives, so it is non value added work. Option A mistakes necessity for value, option C adds a requirement the scenario does not state, and option D treats a support step as value enabling.
  21. The correct answer is A (The 5S method, which organizes the workplace and sustains the gains.): The five steps are sort, set in order, shine, standardize, and sustain, and the scenario follows the first four. Writing a standard for each station is the step that holds the new arrangement in place. Kanban is a pull signal, poka yoke prevents a specific error, and a kaizen blitz is a short focused event rather than a workplace organization method.
  22. The correct answer is A (The theory of constraints, because the system rate follows its slowest step.): The theory of constraints holds that a chain is limited by its weakest link, so process output rises only until the next bottleneck appears. That is what happened after the second triage station was added. Option B concerns sample behavior, option C relates flow, work in process, and time without explaining the moving limit, and option D describes the distribution of averages.
  23. The correct answer is C (Six cards, since 864 units split 150 per card rounds up.): Demand during the replenishment lead time is 240 units per day times 3 days, which equals 720 units. Adding the 20 percent safety factor gives 720 times 1.2, or 864 units. Dividing 864 by the 150 units in a bin gives 5.76, and cards must be whole, so the cell needs six. Options A and B ignore the safety factor or round down, and option D uses a total the data does not support.
  24. The correct answer is A (It tests the factors together and shows how their effects interact.): A factorial design changes the factors together, which lets the analysis estimate main effects and interactions. Testing one factor at a time hides interactions and gives no evidence about combined settings. Option B is false because eight runs are more than seven, option C ignores replication and randomization, and option D describes the one factor at a time approach.
  25. The correct answer is B (Five hours, found by dividing 60 in process by 12 per hour.): Little's law states that work in process equals throughput multiplied by flow time, so flow time equals work in process divided by throughput. Sixty requests in process divided by 12 completions per hour gives five hours. Option A uses a total the data does not support, option C divides the wrong way, and option D inverts the ratio.
  26. The correct answer is D (Look for an assignable cause, because the point signals an unusual event.): A point beyond a control limit is a signal that variation from an assignable cause may be present, so the team investigates that specific subgroup. Adjusting the process at once risks reacting to a single event, and recomputing limits from a signal point hides the evidence. Common cause variation is handled by changing the system, not by chasing individual points.
  27. The correct answer is C (Control limits describe variation, and capability compares that to specs.): Control limits come from the process data and show how much variation common causes produce. Specification limits come from the customer, and capability indices such as Cpk compare the process spread and centering against them. A process can be stable and still fail to meet specifications, so option A reverses the source of the limits, option B changes the chart rather than the process, and option D states a relationship that does not hold.
  28. The correct answer is B (A shift has occurred, so the team should look for a likely cause.): Run rules flag a shift when a set number of consecutive points fall on one side of the median, which indicates non random behavior. Because no planned change was made, the team needs to find what else changed, such as a new supplier, a new operator, or a seasonal effect. Option A ignores the signal, and options C and D assume a chart error without evidence.
  29. The correct answer is A (A p chart, because it tracks a proportion with varying sample size.): The p chart plots the fraction of units that are defective and recalculates its limits for each sample size, which matches a monthly count whose denominator moves. The np chart needs a constant sample size, the c chart needs a constant area of opportunity, and the u chart plots defects per unit when a unit can carry more than one defect. The question is about patients with one injury, so the proportion chart fits.
  30. The correct answer is D (Cpk and Ppk, because they separate within subgroup and overall variation.): Cpk uses the within subgroup standard deviation, which reflects short term capability, while Ppk uses the overall standard deviation from every measurement, which reflects long term performance. Comparing the two shows how much the process drifts or shifts over time. Options A and C draw both indices from the within subgroup data, so they cover only the short term, and option B draws both from the overall data, so it covers only the long term.
  31. The correct answer is C (A control plan that names each measure, its chart, and its owner.): A control plan records what will be monitored after the project closes, including the measurement, the chart, the frequency, the response when a signal appears, and the person accountable. That handover keeps the gain from fading. A charter defines the project, a work instruction describes one task, and a gauge study checks the measurement system.
  32. The correct answer is B (DMADV, because it defines, measures, analyzes, designs, and verifies.): Design for Six Sigma uses DMADV or DMADOV, which begin with customer requirements and then design a new product or process before verifying it. DMAIC improves a process that is already running. PDCA is a general improvement cycle, and RACI is a role assignment tool rather than a design roadmap.
  33. The correct answer is A (Tolerance design that stacks variation statistically, not at worst case.): Tolerance design sets the allowed variation for parts and assemblies, and statistical tolerancing adds the variances of the components instead of assuming that every part sits at its extreme. That approach often allows looser individual tolerances and lower cost while keeping fit acceptable. Design for test adds inspection, design for maintainability eases service, and design for producibility simplifies manufacturing.
  34. The correct answer is C (Score ideas on strategic fit, customer impact, cost of poor quality, and measurable return): Project screening weighs how well a problem connects to the business strategy, how much it matters to customers, how much poor quality costs, and whether the gain can be measured. That mix is the screening logic in the body of knowledge, and it keeps limited Black Belt time on projects with a real payoff. The other options choose projects by how quickly they can be finished, by personal interest, or by headcount alone, and none of those predicts a business result. A fast project can still be trivial, and an interesting project can still fail to move a key measure.
  35. The correct answer is B (The champion, who clears roadblocks and secures resources across departments): In the Six Sigma roles model the champion is the senior manager who sponsors the project, obtains resources, and removes the organizational barriers that a Black Belt cannot clear alone. The Black Belt runs the project work, the finance analyst verifies the claimed savings, and the process owner sustains the new method after handoff. Those roles all matter, yet none of them owns cross department resource decisions. That is why the stalled operator time points to the champion.
  36. The correct answer is A (Use lean methods, because the waste sits in flow and waiting rather than in process variation): Lean targets steps that do not add value, so travel, searching, and handoffs are classic targets for value stream mapping, 5S, and standard work. Six Sigma suits problems where a measured output varies and the cause of that variation is unknown. A hypothesis test or a capability study can describe the delays, but neither one removes the wasted motion and waiting by itself. Planned experiments fit when several factors must be compared, not when the steps are simply in the wrong order.
  37. The correct answer is D (Readiness and stakeholder concerns were skipped, so build support and communication now): Change management asks the team to assess readiness, analyze stakeholders, plan communication, and involve the people who will live with the new method. When a solution fades after the pilot, the usual cause is that the affected staff never owned the change, not that the data was wrong. Retraining in the same way repeats the original mistake, and a shorter pilot would not address the resistance either. Checking the numbers is reasonable housekeeping, but the relapse pattern points to adoption, not measurement.
  38. The correct answer is B (Visiting competitors is competitive benchmarking, and reading studies is best practice benchmarking): Competitive benchmarking compares your own performance and practices directly with competitors, while best practice benchmarking looks at proven methods from any source, including other industries and published studies. Collaborative benchmarking is a voluntary sharing arrangement between organizations, often through a consortium rather than a study that you simply read. Breakthrough benchmarking aims at a large step change in performance and is a goal, not a data gathering type. The other options either swap the two labels or use a category that describes the size of the improvement.
  39. The correct answer is C (Satisfaction is a leading indicator, and complaints are a lagging indicator): A leading indicator moves before the outcome it predicts, so the early drop in satisfaction warns of trouble that shows up later as formal complaints. A lagging indicator records a result after the fact, which is what the complaint total does. The reporting calendar does not decide the label, because a monthly measure can still lead or lag the outcome. Customer opinion is not automatically a leading measure either, since the timing of the change relative to the outcome is what matters.
  40. The correct answer is D (Hard costs appear in the profit and loss statements, and soft benefits are cost avoidance): Hard savings show up as measurable changes in the accounting records, such as lower material spend or reduced labor on the profit and loss statement. Cost avoidance, like overtime that was never spent, is a soft benefit because the money never left the accounts. One time project expenses are simply the cost of the project, not a class of savings. The other options attach the two labels to the wrong sources or to the wrong time frame.
  41. The correct answer is A (Two years and eight months, because forty thousand over fifteen thousand is 2.67): Straight payback divides the initial cost by the annual benefit, so 40,000 divided by 15,000 equals 2.67 years. Two full years recover 30,000, and the remaining 10,000 is recovered in about 0.67 of a year, which is eight months. The four year figure is the benefit period, not the payback point. The one year and four months option divides by two years of benefit at once, which double counts the cash flow.
  42. The correct answer is B (Storming, because conflict about methods and roles surfaces before the team settles): The classic sequence is forming, storming, norming, performing, and adjourning, and open disagreement about methods and roles is the storming stage. Forming features polite uncertainty, while norming brings agreed ways of working and performing brings steady output. Conflict itself is not proof of poor performance, so the argument does not by itself mean the team has reached performing. The Black Belt should surface the disagreement and set ground rules rather than avoid it.
  43. The correct answer is A (Nominal group technique, where members write ideas privately, then discuss them): Nominal group technique asks each member to record ideas alone first, then the group lists, clarifies, and ranks them, which blunts the effect of a dominant voice. Multi-voting and consensus can follow once the list is set, but they work poorly when the list was built in a loud open discussion. Plain brainstorming is fast, yet it also rewards the quickest speaker. A round robin reading of the charter shares information without gathering the team's ranked judgment.
  44. The correct answer is C (A virtual cross functional team, supported by clear communication norms and shared tools): The members come from different functions, which makes the team cross functional, and they work apart in different time zones, which makes it virtual. Virtual teams need explicit communication norms, agreed response times, and shared document tools because casual hallway contact is not available. A functional team stays inside one department and would not cover the three functions. Self directed work relates to who sets the goals, not to how the members are spread or connected.
  45. The correct answer is D (Openness to change, so the team can sustain a new method after the project): The selection criteria in the body of knowledge include the ability to influence, openness to change, needed skills, subject expertise, and availability. Resistance to past changes is a real signal that a member may block adoption, even when that member is highly capable, so the two candidates are not equal on this factor. Skill and process knowledge still count, and a mix of functions is useful, but neither one outweighs a genuine barrier to change. Length of service and meeting attendance are practical details rather than deciding criteria.
  46. The correct answer is A (Sending an agenda in advance and inviting the people who can decide): The body of knowledge names agendas, on time starts, pre work, and the right people and resources as the core meeting practices. An agenda sets the purpose and the time for each item, and including the person who can approve overtime removes the main obstacle before the meeting begins. Recording the session or adding speaking turns would not supply the missing decision authority. Starting on time is useful, yet this meeting also failed because the needed decision maker was absent.
  47. The correct answer is B (A SIPOC map, which frames suppliers, inputs, process steps, outputs, and customers): SIPOC stands for suppliers, inputs, process, outputs, and customers, and it gives a high level boundary view of a process on one page. The team uses it at the start of Define to settle where the process begins and ends before mapping any detail. A Gantt chart schedules project tasks, a RACI matrix assigns decision roles, and a Pareto chart ranks problem types. Each of those answers a different question than the boundary question the sponsor is asking.
  48. The correct answer is D (The Kano model, which separates must be requirements from delighting features): The Kano model sorts features by how their presence or absence affects satisfaction. A must be requirement, sometimes called a dissatisfier, causes complaints when it is missing and earns no extra praise when it is present, which fits login speed. A delighter such as dark mode brings pleasure when it is offered but does not upset users when it is absent. A CTQ tree and a QFD matrix translate needs into measurable or designed requirements, and a SIPOC map shows process boundaries, so none of them explains the two satisfaction patterns.
  49.   54. The correct answer is D (Nominal, nominal, interval, and ordinal, matching the order of the four items): Machine number and operator shift are labels with no order, so both are nominal. Oven temperature in degrees Celsius has equal intervals between degrees, and its zero point is a convention rather than a true absence of heat, so it is interval.   55. The correct answer is A (Stratified sampling, where each department contributes a share matching its size): Stratified sampling splits the population into groups that differ on the measure of interest and then draws from each group in proportion to its size, so the sample mirrors the department mix. Simple random sampling gives every invoice an equal chance, but a single random draw can still land light on a small department. Systematic sampling every fortieth invoice follows the stack order and can drift with the department blocks. Taking the first two hundred invoices is convenience sampling and would overrepresent whichever department sits on top.
  56. The correct answer is C (Reproducibility, because the average readings differ between the two operators): Gauge repeatability and reproducibility studies separate variation within one operator from variation between operators. Repeatability is the spread when one person measures the same part again, and here those repeats line up well, so repeatability looks good. Reproducibility is the shift between operators, and the near millimeter gap points straight at that component. Bias and linearity relate to how far readings sit from a reference value across the range, which this study has not examined.
  57. The correct answer is B (The central limit theorem, which describes the sampling distribution of the mean): The central limit theorem states that the distribution of sample means approaches a normal curve as the sample size grows, even when the individual values come from a skewed population. Thirty values per sample is a common working size for that effect. The law of large numbers addresses how a sample mean settles toward the population mean, not the shape of the means across many samples. Chebyshev's rule makes a distribution free statement about spread, and the multiplication rule combines probabilities.
  58. The correct answer is D (Cp and Cpk are equal at 1.00, so the process is centered on target): Cp compares the specification width with the process spread as 0.60 divided by 0.60, which gives 1.00. Cpk takes centering into account, and it also returns 1.00 because the mean sits exactly at the specification center, leaving 0.30 millimeter or three standard deviations to each limit. When Cp and Cpk agree, the process runs centered, and when they differ, the gap shows the loss from off center running. Cp does not measure centering on its own, because it never uses the process mean.
  59. The correct answer is A (About 72.9 percent, because the three step yields multiply as 0.90 cubed): Rolled throughput yield is the product of the first pass yields of every step in the line. Each step here passes 90 percent of the units that reach it, so the figure is 0.90 times 0.90 times 0.90, which equals 0.729 or 72.9 percent. A single step yield of 90 percent describes only one step and overstates the whole line. Multiplying just two steps gives 81 percent, and using four steps would understate the result because the line has only three.

  61. The correct answer is C (A third factor drives both measures, so the correlation is not causation): A correlation shows that two measures move together, not that one produces the other. Warm weather raises both ice cream sales and swimming, and more swimming raises the chance of drowning, so temperature is a lurking variable behind the pair. Nothing in the story shows a weak coefficient, and a strong one would still not prove cause.   62. The correct answer is D (A type II error, because the test failed to detect a real difference): A type II error, also called a beta error, happens when a test fails to reject a null hypothesis that is false, which is exactly what the first test did. Its counterpart, the type I error, rejects a true null hypothesis and is controlled by the significance level alpha. Power is the chance of detecting an effect that is truly there, so a low power test is what allowed the miss. The larger trial succeeded because it gathered more evidence, not because the first test made an alpha error.
  63. The correct answer is A (385, because 1.96 squared times 0.25 divided by 0.05 squared is 384.16): For a proportion, the standard sample size formula is z squared times p times one minus p, divided by the margin of error squared. With ninety five percent confidence z is 1.96, and the most demanding case for an unknown share is p of 0.5, so p times one minus p is 0.25. That gives 3.8416 times 0.25 divided by 0.0025, which equals 384.16, and rounding up gives 385. The 768 option substitutes 0.5 for the whole variance term, and the 196 option confuses the z value with the sample size.

  66. The correct answer is C (At least one lot mean differs, and a follow up test can locate it): A one way analysis of variance tests the null hypothesis that all group means are equal. With a p value of 0.02 and an alpha of 0.05, the null hypothesis is rejected, so at least one lot mean differs from the others.   83. The correct answer is A (Statistical tolerancing, which combines part variation using the expected spread): Statistical tolerancing treats each part dimension as a distribution rather than assuming every part sits at its limit at the same time. The combined spread is smaller than the sum of the worst case limits, which often allows wider part tolerances and lower cost while the assembly still fits. Worst case tolerancing adds the limits in the same direction and is deliberately conservative. Design for maintainability and design for test are design constraints about service and checking, not about how part variations combine.

The distribution does not stay skewed at large sample sizes. It does not copy the population shape at every size, and it becomes narrower rather than wider as the sample grows.

A hypothesis test does not prove a difference, and the chance of detecting a real difference is the power, which the p value does not report.

The correct answer is D (Use the diagram to select candidate causes and then test them with data.): A cause and effect diagram organizes possible causes into categories so the team can choose the most likely ones and confirm them with evidence before changing the process. Every listed cause is a hypothesis rather than a confirmed root cause. The diagram is not a control chart, and deleting categories that hold fewer causes removes possible explanations before any data have been collected.
  117. The correct answer is C (The first item, because a severity of 8 signals a more serious potential effect.): The first item has a risk priority number of 8 times 4 times 5, which equals 160, and the second item has 3 times 9 times 2, which equals 54, so the first item also ranks higher on the combined score, and its severity of 8 means the effect could be far more serious. The second item has the lower risk priority number, so the first option is wrong. A detection score of 5 is not the largest score on the sheet, and a high occurrence score alone does not outweigh a much lower severity.
  118. The correct answer is A (About 0.08, because the two event probabilities are added together.): An OR gate fails when either input occurs, and for two independent events the exact rule is 0.05 + 0.03 minus 0.05 times 0.03, which equals 0.08 minus 0.0015, so the result is 0.0785 and the simple addition gives a close answer that rounds to about 0.08. The product of the two events, 0.0015, describes an AND gate rather than an OR gate. Neither the larger nor the smaller event alone sets the top event probability.
  119. The correct answer is D (Motion and waiting, because the travel and the key delay add no value.): Walking 60 meters for supplies is motion waste, and standing while a key is found is waiting waste, so this step carries two of the seven wastes at the same time. Overproduction is making or moving more than the next step needs. Inventory is material sitting idle, and over-processing is doing more work to the item than the customer values, so none of those describes travel and delay.
  120. The correct answer is B (Randomization, which spreads the effect of uncontrolled factors across the runs.): Randomizing the run order keeps an outside event, such as the weekend oven service, from lining up with one factor level, so its effect does not masquerade as a treatment effect. Replication repeats treatment combinations but does not fix a run order that confounds time with temperature. Blocking handles known sources of variation by grouping similar units, and power describes the sensitivity of the test rather than the order of the runs.

  122.   127. Points inside the limits do not by themselves prove stability when a run rule is tripped. Long runs on one side are not a normal feature of a stable process, and widening the limits hides the signal instead of removing its cause.
  128. Waiting gives the cause more time to act, and comparing the point with the specification limits answers a different question, because control limits come from the process itself.
  129. The correct answer is B (Mixing different times inside one subgroup hides shifts in the process.): Rational subgrouping builds subgroups from units made close together in time so that the spread within a subgroup reflects common cause variation, and a subgroup that spans a whole shift averages away any shift in the process. The sample size is not the issue here. Cost is not the principle at stake, and control limits are calculated from the data rather than widened because a subgroup mixes several time periods.
  130. An individuals chart is for continuous measurements rather than counts.

The charter and the Gantt chart belong to project management rather than ongoing control. An affinity diagram organizes customer comments and plays no part in holding a process in place.

  133. The first option describes only the safety of worst case and misses the cost it carries. Statistical tolerancing does not add the tolerances directly, and the two methods give different results even when the parts come from a stable process.

  157.   159. The five readings average 10.2, which equals the reference value, so bias is near zero for this setup. Repeatability describes the spread among the readings, and linearity describes how error changes across the range, while stability describes drift over time.   160. The correct answer is D (Cpk is 1.33, because the mean sits off center and the tighter side counts): The upper half of the tolerance is 505 minus 501, which is 4, and dividing by three sigma gives 4 divided by 3, or 1.33. The lower half is 501 minus 495, which is 6, giving 6 divided by 3, or 2.00. Cpk takes the smaller of the two, so the answer is 1.33, which reflects the off center mean. Option A repeats Cp, which ignores centering, and B takes the larger ratio instead of the smaller one.
  161. The correct answer is A (2,000 defects per million, which is far from the six sigma level): Defects per million opportunities equals the defect count divided by the number of opportunities, multiplied by one million. Here 500 divided by 250,000 is 0.002, and 0.002 times 1,000,000 is 2,000. A rate of 2,000 defects per million sits well above the 3.4 defects per million linked to the six sigma level under the usual 1.5 sigma shift. Options B and D understate the rate, B by a factor of ten and D by a factor of forty, and C repeats the raw count without scaling it.
  162. The correct answer is C (Binomial for the defective parts, and Poisson for the scratches on the panel): The binomial distribution fits a count of successes in a fixed number of trials where each trial is good or bad, which matches 100 parts with a pass or fail result. The Poisson distribution fits counts of events in an area, volume, or time when the number of chances is not fixed, which matches scratches on a panel. The normal distribution models continuous measurements, and the exponential distribution models waiting times. The pairing in C matches each count to its generating process.
  163. The correct answer is A (The link is strong and negative, but staffing levels may not cause the complaints): A correlation of minus 0.82 is strong and negative, meaning stores with more staff tend to have fewer complaints. Correlation alone cannot show that one thing causes the other, because store size, service model, or customer mix could drive both values. Cutting staff further might remove the people who resolve complaints, which could raise the count instead of lowering it. The manager needs a designed test or more evidence before acting on the link.
  164. The correct answer is B (The chart shows a strong positive link, and a designed experiment would confirm cause): The points rise steadily with little scatter, which shows a strong positive association between line speed and fill weight. Association is not proof of cause, because a third factor such as product temperature could move both values, and only a designed experiment can separate the effect. Option A treats the pattern as proof, C claims there is no relationship, and D reverses the direction of the link. The careful reading is a strong link that still needs a designed test.
  165. The correct answer is C (The result is significant at 0.05, so the team rejects the null hypothesis): The p value is the chance of seeing data at least this extreme if the null hypothesis were true, and 0.03 is below the 0.05 alpha, so the team rejects the null. A p value of 0.03 does not mean there is a 3 percent chance that the null is true, which is a common misreading. It also does not prove that the seal cuts leaks by exactly 3 percent, because the size of the effect comes from the data rather than the p value. Option D reverses the comparison rule.
  166. The correct answer is D (A type II error, since the test failed to detect a real problem): A type II error, also called beta, happens when a test fails to detect a real difference and a bad batch passes. A type I error, also called alpha, is the opposite case where a good batch is wrongly rejected, which is the first test in the stem. Alpha and beta are the probabilities of those two errors rather than the errors themselves, so C uses loose wording. Because the bad batch was missed, the second case is the type II error.
  167. The correct answer is A (Machines, because no causes are listed under that branch): The fishbone sorts causes into branches, and this diagram carries entries for Methods, People, Environment, Measurement, and Materials. Only the Machines branch holds no entries, so the team has not examined machine or equipment causes. Options B, C, and D name branches that already hold entries in the diagram. Checking the empty branch keeps the search from narrowing too soon.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Mode two, because its risk priority number of 240 is the larger one): The risk priority number multiplies severity, occurrence, and detection, so mode one scores 7 times 3 times 4, or 84, and mode two scores 5 times 8 times 6, or 240. A larger risk priority number places a failure mode higher in the queue, so mode two goes first. Option A leans on the higher severity score, which is worth watching but does not set the order by itself, and C names the smaller number. Option D points to the lower severity, which is not a reason to go first.
  2. The correct answer is C (The slipping belt is the root cause, and the fuse is only a symptom): Root cause analysis keeps asking why until it reaches the condition that, once fixed, stops the problem from returning. In this chain the fuse blows because the motor overheats, the motor overheats because the fan stalls, and the fan stalls because the belt slips, so the belt is the root cause. The fuse failing is a symptom that repeats because the underlying condition remains. Option A stops at the symptom, and B treats each answer as a separate cause.
  3. The correct answer is D (Temperature drives the response, so the team should focus on that factor): A main effect is the change in the average response when one factor moves from its low level to its high level, averaged over the levels of the other factor. Temperature shows a 6 degree shift while pressure shows 1 degree, so temperature has the larger effect on the response. A small interaction means the effect of temperature holds at both pressure levels, which supports focusing on temperature. Options A, B, and C ignore the size of the two main effects.
  4. The correct answer is B (It allows main effects and two factor interactions to be estimated free of each other): Resolution tells you which effects are clear of which others, and resolution five keeps main effects clear of two factor interactions and keeps two factor interactions clear of one another. A sixteen run design with five factors at resolution five is a standard screening choice when interactions between pairs may matter. Option A drops the two factor interactions, which the design can still estimate, and C claims three factor interactions that stay aliased in this design. Option D describes a full factorial rather than the fractional design in use.
  5. The correct answer is A (Motion and waiting, since the walk and tray delay are addressed): Motion is the waste of people moving around, which the far storeroom causes, and waiting is idle time, which the tray delay causes. The new layout shortens the walk, and a kanban for gloves keeps a small supply near the point of use. Reheating a plate is a form of waiting or extra processing rather than a defect, so D mislabels that waste. Overproduction and inventory are not described, so B and C miss the two problems in the stem.
  6. The correct answer is C (Mistake proofing, since the design blocks the wrong label): Mistake proofing, also called poka yoke, changes the process so the wrong action is hard to perform. Making the tray fit one label stock removes the chance of feeding the wrong sheet, which is a classic mechanical device. Standard work documents the best sequence but does not stop the wrong label, and an andon is a signal that a problem has already appeared. Leveling the workload is a different lean idea, so B does not fit.
  7. The correct answer is D (About 48 percent, because availability, speed, and quality all reduce the result): Availability is 340 running minutes out of 400 planned minutes, or 0.85. Performance is 2,000 parts divided by the 3,400 parts the design rate allows in 340 minutes, or about 0.588. Quality is 1,900 good parts out of 2,000, or 0.95. Multiplying 0.85 by 0.588 by 0.95 gives about 0.475, or 48 percent. Each single factor overstates the result because the measure combines all three.
  8. The correct answer is B (Smaller, more frequent replenishment replaces the large buffer held for the weekly gap): A pull system uses a signal to replace only what has been used, so daily delivery shrinks the stock needed to cover demand. The plant no longer has to hold three weeks of parts against a weekly shipment, because the gap it was insuring against has closed. The price of the part did not change, and the line did not slow, so A and D describe changes that are not in the stem. Cards signal replenishment of a small quantity rather than one large batch, which rules out C.
  9. The correct answer is A (Keep the tank running without idle time and feed it good parts): The first step at a constraint is to exploit it, which means keeping it busy and feeding it work that will not be rejected. A constraint loses output for every idle minute and for every bad part that consumes its time. Speeding the upstream machines builds more inventory in front of the tank, and buying a second tank is a later and costly step. Slowing upstream to match the tank keeps the constraint busy but does not raise the throughput it delivers.
  10. The correct answer is C (An np chart, since it tracks the number of defective units per constant sample): The np chart plots the number of defective units in a sample, and it needs a constant sample size, which the daily batch of 200 provides. A p chart tracks the proportion defective, a c chart tracks the count of defects in a constant area or unit, and a u chart tracks defects per unit when the sample varies. Option A describes an individuals chart, which fits continuous measurements over time. Because the team counts defective invoices in equal batches, the np chart is the match.
  11. The correct answer is D (Added variation, because reacting to common cause variation amounts to tampering): When a chart is stable, the variation comes from the system itself, and adjusting the process on ordinary movement adds variation instead of removing it. This practice is called tampering, and it often makes the process less predictable than it was. Tightening the limits on a stable chart would also raise false alarms, because control limits describe what the process normally does. Adjusting the process cannot reduce variation that the system itself produces.
  12. The correct answer is A (A shift, because eight straight points above the median is a nonrandom pattern): A run of eight points on one side of the median is one of the standard run chart signals of a shift in the process. The first 12 weeks bounce around the median, and the final eight all sit above it, so the pattern is not random noise. Points inside specification limits can still carry a signal, which is why option B is not the right reading. The last four points are part of the longer run, and a short run alone is weaker evidence than the eight point signal.
  13. The correct answer is B (A control plan, which names the measures, the charts, and the responses): A control plan is the handover document that lists what to measure, how often to measure it, which chart to use, and what to do when the chart signals. It keeps the gains in place after the project team disbands, which is what the sponsor wants. A charter sets the problem and goal at the start, and a value stream map shows flow and time. A measurement system study checks the gauge rather than the ongoing control of the process.
  14. The correct answer is C (Specification limits come from the customer, and control limits come from the process data): Specification limits carry the voice of the customer and mark what is acceptable, while control limits are calculated from the process data and describe what the process normally does. Here the mean is 100 with a standard deviation of 0.5, so control limits at 98.5 and 101.5 sit three standard deviations from the mean. The customer limits of 98 and 102 sit wider than the control limits, which shows the process has room to vary before defects occur. The other options mix up the two sources or claim that both sets move together.
  15. The correct answer is D (DMADV, because the roadmap designs a new process from customer needs): DMADV stands for define, measure, analyze, design, and verify, and it is used when a product or process does not exist yet. The roadmap starts from customer needs, builds a design that can meet them, and then verifies the design, which fits a new mobile app. DMAIC improves an existing process, so it does not match a service that has no current process to measure. Option C names the right roadmap but describes the wrong starting point.
  16. The correct answer is B (Robust design, which makes the design less sensitive to the sources of variation): Robust design sets the design so that normal variation in inputs, such as wire thickness and heat treatment, has less effect on the output force. Tolerance design tightens the allowed range of each input instead, which usually raises cost. Design for test adds test points, and design for maintainability shortens repair time, so neither one addresses sensitivity to input variation. Because the goal is a design that holds its target across the two sources, robust design is the fit.
  17. The correct answer is A (Add contingency plans that name backup suppliers and trigger points for switching): Contingency planning means naming the events that could break the plan, such as losing a single supplier, and deciding in advance what the team will do. Building a backup supplier list and a clear trigger for switching keeps the improvement work moving when the risk becomes real. Waiting for a longer contract leaves the plan exposed, deleting the risk from the charter does not remove it from the world, and reopening the plan later only works after the damage is done.
  18. The correct answer is D (The champion who sponsors the project and clears the barriers): The body of knowledge places the champion and the sponsor in the role that provides resources, removes roadblocks, and supports the project at a level above the team. When departments withhold time or decisions, that sponsor has the standing to settle the conflict and free the team. A Green Belt, an operator, and a finance analyst each contribute to the work, but none of them holds the authority that this roadblock needs.
  19. The correct answer is C (A lack of management support that leaves teams without real resources): The body of knowledge names weak management support and missing resources as common causes of Six Sigma failure, and taking trained people away for urgent work removes the very capacity the program was built on. The training already exists, so a missing-training cause does not fit, and neither an overuse of control charts nor a broad idea portfolio explains why finished projects are so few.
  20. The correct answer is A (Match the message and the timing to each group's influence and concerns): A stakeholder analysis tells the team who can block the change, who must carry it out, and what each group cares about, so the communication plan can be aimed at the right people with the right message at the right time. Sending one message to everyone, leading with the largest group, or handing the announcement to the most senior group all ignore the differences the analysis just revealed.
  21. The correct answer is C (Competitive benchmarking, because the comparison is against a direct rival): Benchmarking types are named by who is being studied. A study of a direct competitor is competitive benchmarking, a study of another unit inside the same organization is internal, a study with a willing partner that shares data is collaborative, and breakthrough benchmarking aims at a step change rather than a like-for-like rival. The scenario names a rival firm, so only the competitive label is correct.
  22. The correct answer is D (Open safety walk findings, because they predict future injuries): A leading indicator moves before the result it is meant to influence, so hazards found on a safety walk today point to injuries that could happen later. The injury rate itself is the lagging result that arrives at the end of the quarter. On-time delivery is a service measure with no direct link to safety, and the two labels are not interchangeable.
  23. The correct answer is D (A hard benefit, because the scrap cost sits on the profit statement): A hard benefit is one that can be traced to the profit and loss statement and verified by finance, and scrap cost recorded there meets that test. Volume uncertainty does not turn a recorded cost into a soft benefit, and scrap is not merely an internal score. The scrap cost already sits on the profit statement, so the option that calls the saving untraceable to that statement contradicts the facts.
  24. The correct answer is D (They create a line of sight from branch work to the bank goals): A line of sight means every local measure can be traced upward to a goal the organization has set, so staff can see how their daily work supports the strategy. The branch measures add detail below the top goals, so they do not replace them. Comparing staff effort or ranking branches does not connect the work to the goals, which is the purpose being asked about.
  25. The correct answer is B (A virtual team that meets by video across the time zones): A virtual team is the design built for members who are separated by geography and cannot travel, using shared tools and scheduled remote meetings to do the work. A team that visits each plant in turn conflicts with the frozen travel budget, drawing members from only one plant leaves the other sites unrepresented, and hiring a contractor is not a team design.
  26. The correct answer is A (Influence and openness to change, because the project needs both): The body of knowledge lists ability to influence and openness to change among the factors that make a team member effective, and this project must change long standing habits on the night shift. Seniority, equal availability, and a fair rotation order are simple rules, but none of them predicts whether the new documentation steps will actually be adopted.
  27. The correct answer is C (The team has reached storming, and the conflict can be worked through): Storming is the stage where members test each other, push different views, and sort out how the work will be done, so open argument about method is a normal part of the journey. Norming and performing come later, once roles and ground rules settle. The team has not failed, and stepping in to settle the dispute personally would skip the work the group needs to do.
  28. The correct answer is D (A nominal group technique with silent writing, then round robin sharing): The nominal group technique asks members to write ideas or ratings on their own before anyone speaks, then collects them in turn, which reduces the pull of the most senior voices. An open voice vote and a short open debate both let the same two people dominate, and a decision by the senior members with a review leaves the rest of the team with little real say.
  29. The correct answer is A (Groupthink, because members withheld doubts to keep the team in agreement): Groupthink shows up when a team values harmony so much that members hold back concerns and the group records agreement that no one truly feels. The scenario describes exactly that silence followed by private doubt. The team did not wander to another topic, the meeting did not run long, and no single member is described as dominating the discussion.
  30. The correct answer is C (The transport aide who receives the patient after the order): An internal customer is the next step or the next person inside the organization who receives the output of the process, so the transport aide who takes over once the order is written fits that definition. The patient, the insurer, and the regulator are all affected by the process, but they sit outside the organization and are external customers.
  31. The correct answer is A (A focus group of eight users who abandoned the same screen): The survey can show how many users left and what they say, but a focus group lets the team ask follow-up questions and hear the reasons behind the behavior, which adds the depth the Black Belt is asking for. A larger survey adds more of the same responses, click logs show where users stopped rather than why, and a rival benchmark studies another company instead of these users.
  32. The correct answer is A (Set a target and a tolerance for a measurable delivery temperature): A CTQ tree moves from the broad customer wish at the top, to the requirements that satisfy it, to a measurable characteristic with a target and an allowed range at the bottom. Temperature with a target and a tolerance is that measurable step. Listing routes, ranking drivers, and comparing rivals are useful activities, but none of them converts the wish into a measurable requirement.
  33. The correct answer is B (Claim errors run at 8 percent, and the goal is 3 percent by June): A strong problem statement names the measure, the current baseline, the target, and the time frame, so that anyone reading it can tell what success will look like and when. The other statements describe a feeling, a customer complaint, or an instruction to work harder, and none of them gives a baseline, a target, or a date.
  34. The correct answer is C (Park the ideas in a separate list and keep the current scope): A charter defines the boundary of the project, and widening it midstream puts the original goal and the timeline at risk, so good practice is to record the new ideas for later review and finish what was agreed. Quietly expanding, extending the timeline, or abandoning the original goal all break the scope discipline that makes the project measurable.
  35. The correct answer is B (A RACI model that names who is responsible and consulted): The RACI model assigns each task a responsible person, an accountable owner, the people to consult, and the people to inform, which is exactly the role clarity the team is missing. A Gantt chart shows timing, a work breakdown structure divides the work, and an activity network shows dependencies, and none of those three names the people and their roles.
  36. The correct answer is A (The scale is ordinal, so the gaps between ratings are not equal): Poor, fair, good, and excellent put the ratings in order, but the distance from poor to fair need not equal the distance from good to excellent, so an average can mislead. A nominal scale would only name categories, an interval scale would have equal gaps without a true zero, and a ratio scale would need a true zero, which this rating scale does not have.
  37. The correct answer is B (Systematic sampling, because a fixed interval selects the files): Systematic sampling picks a random start and then takes every kth item from the list, which is exactly the pattern described. Stratified sampling divides the population into groups first, cluster sampling selects whole groups, and simple random sampling gives every file an equal chance with no fixed interval, so the interval is the clue that names the method.
  38.
