The upper side has room, because no readings reach above 103. Moving the mean up a little uses that room and cuts the risk at the low end, while widening the limits hides the problem, reducing the mean pushes more weight toward the limit, and taking no action leaves the low tail where it is.
  39. The correct answer is B (Operators differ in how they measure, so training or a fixture is needed): Repeatability describes how well one operator repeats a measurement, while reproducibility describes how closely operators agree with each other, so good repeatability with poor reproducibility points at the people and their method. The parts are not described as a problem, the sample of fifty parts measured twice is the standard gauge study pattern, and a measurement system that fails reproducibility is not fit for capability work yet.
  40. The correct answer is A (The spread fits the limits, but the process is off center): Cp compares the width of the process to the width of the specification, and a Cp of 1.60 means the spread is comfortably narrow, while Cpk also counts how far the mean sits from the nearer limit. The gap between the two numbers is caused by the off center mean, not by a wide spread. The process is not centered, and the sample size is not what creates the difference between the two indices.
  41. The correct answer is A (1,000 DPMO, found as 12 divided by 12,000 opportunities): Total opportunities equal 4,000 calls times 3 opportunities per call, which is 12,000. DPMO is defects per million opportunities, so 12 divided by 12,000 equals 0.001, and 0.001 times 1,000,000 equals 1,000 DPMO. Multiplying 12 by 1,000, dividing calls by defects, and dividing defects by calls each use the wrong base and give a number that cannot be compared with other processes.
  42. The correct answer is C (Night shift hold times run higher than day shift hold times): The night shift median of 15 minutes sits well above the day shift median of 8 minutes, and the whole night box sits above the day box, so the night shift runs higher on this measure. The boxes do not share a center or a spread, no outliers are plotted for either shift, and the day box, drawn from 5 to 10 minutes, is wider than the night box, drawn from 13 to 17 minutes.
  43. The correct answer is D (They show traceability to a standard, so readings can be trusted): Calibration links a measuring device to a known reference standard, and the record shows when that link was last checked and how far the device had drifted, which is what makes the readings trustworthy. The wrench is never the only source of variation, a check cannot be removed simply by recording it, and the operators still affect the measurement system, so the records cannot replace their study.
  44. The correct answer is B (A strong association exists, but causation needs more evidence): A correlation of 0.82 describes a strong linear association between the two variables, and it does not by itself show that one causes the other. A hidden factor such as experience could drive both. The statement that training caused the gain and the claim that the correlation proves cause both go beyond the statistic, and the claim that the variables are unrelated ignores the strong positive value.
  45. The correct answer is C (There is about a 5 percent chance the team made a type I error): Alpha is the long run chance of rejecting a true null hypothesis, so at alpha of 0.05 a rejection carries about a 5 percent risk of that type I error. A p-value is not the chance that the null hypothesis is true, so the 3 percent statement is wrong, and alpha does not set the type II error risk, which is beta. Rejecting the null still carries the risk that alpha describes.
  46. The correct answer is D (Thickness tends to rise with temperature, and the association looks strong): A band of points running from the lower left to the upper right shows that thickness tends to increase as oven temperature increases, and the tightness of the band suggests a strong linear association. A scatter plot shows association rather than proof of cause, so the claim about the only cause goes too far, the falling direction reads the slope backwards, and the claim of no relationship ignores the clear upward pattern.
  47. The correct answer is A (A one-way ANOVA comparing the three machine means at once): One-way analysis of variance compares the means of three or more groups on one continuous response, which matches three machines and a measured dimension. A chi-square test works on counts or categories, a paired t test compares two related groups on a continuous measure, and a test of proportions needs a proportion as the response, so none of those three fits this data.
  48. The correct answer is C (A chi-square goodness-of-fit test against equal day counts): The data are counts of errors placed into seven day categories, and the question asks whether those counts match an even split, which is exactly what a goodness-of-fit test checks. A test of independence needs two categorical variables in a two-way table, a two-sample t test compares two means, and an ANOVA compares group means on a continuous response, so none of those matches counts in seven categories.
  49. The correct answer is C (Test the listed causes with data and narrow down the likely drivers): A cause and effect diagram organizes possible causes so the team can investigate them, and the next step is to gather data and confirm which candidates really drive the problem. Fixing the first cause that feels likely skips the confirmation, fixing every cause at once wastes effort, and treating the fullest branch as the answer assumes that the number of notes predicts the cause.
  50. The correct answer is B (The severity 8 mode, because severity carries the greatest weight): The risk priority number multiplies severity, occurrence, and detection, so the two modes both score 160 even though one can cause far more harm. Standard practice gives extra weight to severity, especially at high levels, so the severity 8 mode is addressed first even though its occurrence is lower. Equal products do not mean equal risk, and neither occurrence alone nor a simple tie rule replaces that judgement.
  51. The correct answer is C (A ranked list of the gaps between current and target performance): A gap analysis compares the current state with the future state using set measures, then lists where the two differ so the team can aim its work at the largest and most important gaps. A single number hides those differences, a list of people turns a process question into a blame question, and a control chart shows whether the process is stable rather than what must change to reach the target.
  52. The correct answer is D (It misses interactions, so a designed experiment answers the question better): Changing one factor at a time shows the effect of each factor while the other is held still, but it cannot show an interaction, where the best temperature depends on the time. A factorial design varies the factors together and estimates both main effects and the interaction. One-factor-at-a-time does not use fewer runs to reach the same answer, is not the only valid method, and does not match a full factorial.
  53. The correct answer is C (Blocking, so each day forms a group that shares the same raw batch): Blocking groups the runs that share a known source of variation, here the raw material batch from each day, so that the blend effect is not mixed up with the day effect. Confounding would merge the day effect into the blend effect, which is what the team wants to avoid. Repetition repeats a measurement without removing the day effect, and randomization spreads unknown nuisance factors but cannot separate a known day effect when only one batch is available each day.
  54. The correct answer is A (Inventory, because unused files sit waiting and tie up space): Inventory is material or information that sits waiting to be used, and three days of files stacked at every room with most returning untouched matches that waste. Motion concerns people moving, over-processing concerns extra steps added to the work, and defects concern work that must be redone, so each of those describes a different waste than the one in the scenario.
  55. The correct answer is B (Value-added time is a small share, so wait time is the main target): Value-added work totals 22 minutes out of a 180 minute lead time, which is about 12 percent, so the waiting and rework that make up the other 158 minutes dominate the process. That makes the delay between steps the largest opportunity. Value-added time does not dominate, the steps are not balanced, and the lead time is far larger than the value-added time.
  56. The correct answer is C (Use kanban signals to build only what the next stage consumes): A pull system starts production from a signal that the next stage has used up what it needs, so kanban cards or bins tie building to real downstream demand. A bigger buffer pushes more unwanted parts into storage, better forecasts keep the push logic in place, and larger batches build even more of what the next stage may not want.
  57. The correct answer is D (5S for the tool piles and poka-yoke for the connector): 5S sorts, sets in order, and standardizes the tool location so that the right tool is easy to find, and poka-yoke changes the connector or the fixture so it can only go in the correct way. The other pairs mix in kanban, heijunka, SMED, and kaizen, which address flow, leveling, changeover time, and small improvements rather than the two problems on this bench.
  58. The correct answer is B (About 78.5 percent, found by multiplying the three percentages together): Overall equipment effectiveness multiplies availability, performance, and quality, so 0.92 times 0.88 equals 0.8096, and 0.8096 times 0.97 equals 0.7853, which is about 78.5 percent. Averaging gives about 92.3 percent, using only availability and performance gives about 81.0 percent, and quoting the performance rate alone gives 88.0 percent, and each of those drops or double counts a factor.
  59. The correct answer is A (An np chart, because the sample size is constant and units are counted): An np chart plots the number of defective units in a sample of fixed size, which matches a steady sample of 200 invoices classified as correct or incorrect. A p chart tracks the proportion and is used when the sample size changes, an X-bar and R chart tracks a measured variable rather than a count, and a c chart counts defects in a unit rather than defective units.
  60. The correct answer is B (Tampering will add variation, because common cause changes are chased): When a process is stable, the points inside the limits come from common causes that belong to the system, and reacting to each small movement adds adjustments that were not needed. That tampering increases variation instead of reducing it. Tightening the limits does not make variation fall, does not raise the mean, and does not leave the process unchanged, because the operator would be reacting to every point.
  61. The correct answer is D (A run of eight points on one side signals a shift worth study): Standard control chart analysis uses runs rules as well as points beyond the limits, and eight points in a row on one side of the center line is a signal that the process mean has shifted. Every point here sits inside the 47.0 to 53.0 limits, so the out of limit rule does not apply. Moving the center line to follow the points hides the shift, and comparing with the specification limits is not how a control chart signal is judged.
  62. The correct answer is D (A total productive maintenance plan with scheduled filter checks): Total productive maintenance builds routine cleaning, inspection, and replacement into the operator's work so that equipment keeps running without a supervisor watching it, which is what a scheduled filter check provides. A tighter specification band changes the product requirement rather than the machine care, a larger spare parts store only speeds the repair, and a weekly reminder still depends on someone remembering.
  63. The correct answer is B (A control plan that lists measures, limits, and response actions): A control plan records the measure to track, the limits that matter, the frequency of checking, and the action to take when a limit is crossed, and it is the document that carries responsibility from the project team to the process owner. The charter records the original problem, a lessons learned memo looks backward, and a Gantt chart shows task dates rather than how to run the improved process.
  64. The correct answer is A (A smaller process spread means the gauge must resolve finer differences): As a process improves, its spread shrinks, and a gauge that was good enough for the old spread may be too coarse to resolve the new one, so the study is repeated to confirm the measurement system still suits the job. The old study cannot simply be reused, gauge wear does not explain the request, and there is no reason to assume the original study was biased.
  65. The correct answer is C (Use DMADV, since the service does not exist yet and must be designed): Define, measure, analyze, design, and validate is the design for Six Sigma roadmap used when a process or product does not yet exist and must be created to meet customer requirements. DMAIC improves a process that is already running, so it does not fit a service that has not been built. DMADOV is a design roadmap as well, but the option reverses its phases by placing optimization before design.
  66. The correct answer is D (Statistical tolerancing, which combines part distributions instead of worst cases): The scenario describes worst case tolerancing, where every part is assumed to sit at its extreme limit at the same time, which guarantees a fit but forces tight and costly tolerances. Statistical tolerancing uses the distributions of the part dimensions and the way they combine, so the same fit can often be held with looser individual tolerances. Worst case tolerancing is the current method, and design for test and design for maintainability address other design goals.
  67. The correct answer is C (A balanced set of financial, customer, process, and learning measures with targets): A deployment dashboard works when it shows results across the whole business rather than one narrow slice, so leaders can see tradeoffs and where to act. A balanced set of measures groups results into financial, customer, process, and learning views and pairs each one with a target. A project list, training hours, and idea counts are useful details, but they do not show whether the deployment is delivering results.
  68. The correct answer is A (Competitive benchmarking against a direct peer in the same industry): Competitive benchmarking compares an organization with a direct peer that performs the same work, which is what a hospital comparing itself against another hospital is doing. Internal benchmarking compares units inside one organization, functional benchmarking looks outside the industry at a similar function, and generic benchmarking studies a process with no industry link. The team is studying a direct peer, so the other three labels do not fit.
  69. The correct answer is B (Project Y, because it returns the most benefit for each Black Belt used): Divide each benefit by the number of Black Belts the project needs. Project X returns 400,000 divided by 2, which is 200,000 dollars per Belt. Project Y returns 250,000 divided by 1, which is 250,000 dollars per Belt. Project Z returns 500,000 divided by 3, which is about 166,700 dollars per Belt. Project Y gives the largest return for each scarce Belt, even though Project Z has the largest total return.
  70. The correct answer is D (The champion removes barriers and secures the resources the project needs): The champion is a senior manager who owns the project at the leadership level, clears organizational obstacles, and makes sure the team has people, time, and money. Running meetings and collecting data are team leader duties, and the measurement work belongs to the Black Belt and the team. Writing reports and presenting to finance can be shared, but that is not what defines the champion role.
  71. The correct answer is A (900,000 dollars, the internal and external failure costs of the bank): Cost of poor quality is the money spent because things went wrong, which is the internal and external failure cost. Appraisal and prevention costs are the cost of good quality, the money spent to keep defects from happening or to find them early. Adding those two to the failure cost gives 1,350,000 dollars, but that larger figure is the whole cost of quality rather than the cost of poor quality.
  72. The correct answer is C (Takt time is two minutes, so the line is too slow to meet demand): Takt time is the available work time divided by customer demand, so 480 minutes divided by 240 units equals 2.0 minutes per unit. The line takes 3 minutes per unit, which is longer than the 2 minute takt, so it falls behind demand and cannot ship 240 units in the shift without overtime or a faster rate. A takt of three minutes would match the current line, but it would not match the demand.
  73. The correct answer is D (Assign a named manager who works in the process and owns its results): Process ownership belongs with a manager who runs the work every day, is accountable for its measures, and can keep the new standards in place after the project ends. A Black Belt moves on to other projects, the quality department supports the work rather than running it, and a project team disbands at closure. A named line manager keeps the process under daily control.
  74. The correct answer is B (A soft saving, because the money was avoided rather than removed from spend): Hard savings come from the profit and loss statement and are money that no longer leaves the business, while soft savings are cost avoidance that is real but harder to see on the ledger. Avoiding two temporary hires is cost avoidance, so finance would usually report it as a soft benefit until the budget is formally reduced. Rework reduction can lower unit cost, but the classification hinges on whether the spend actually disappeared.
  75. The correct answer is C (Storming, because conflict about roles and direction has surfaced openly): Storming is the stage where members test the leader, push back on the charter, and compete for roles, which matches the arguments described. Forming is the earlier polite stage, norming comes after the team settles its working agreements, and adjourning happens when the work is complete and the team disbands. Naming the stage helps the leader coach the team rather than avoid the conflict.
  76. The correct answer is A (The facilitator guides the meeting process while the leader owns the content): A facilitator manages how the group works, keeping the agenda, drawing out quiet members, and handling conflict, while the technical or project leader owns the substance and the decisions. Keeping those duties separate stops one person from steering both the discussion and the outcome. Recording notes, setting budgets, and approving designs are separate responsibilities.
  77. The correct answer is D (Use nominal group technique so members rank ideas privately, then discuss): Nominal group technique asks members to write and rank ideas on their own before the group talks, which reduces the pull of a dominant talker and gives quiet members equal weight. The written ranks are then combined and discussed, so the team decides with input from everyone. A seniority call or a quick hand vote can let the loudest or highest ranking voice decide, and open debate alone can be steered by the most forceful speaker.
  78. The correct answer is B (Have the team agree on ground rules for meetings, roles, and commitments): Ground rules are set at the start so the team writes its own expectations for attendance, start times, participation, and follow through, which is much easier than correcting habits months later. The team also confirms roles and assignments in that same session. Waiting for problems, adding a sponsor to every meeting, or handing out presentation topics does not build the shared expectations the team needs.
  79. The correct answer is C (Measure the test results on the floor before and after the training): The strongest evidence that training changed behavior is a before and after measure of the actual work, taken on the floor where the procedure is used. End of day surveys, completion records, and supervisor opinions show that people attended and felt good about it, but they do not prove the procedure is being followed or that results improved. A pre and post measure ties the training to the outcome the business cares about.
  80. The correct answer is D (One-on-one interviews with customers who recently stopped partway through the form): Interviews let the team ask follow up questions and hear the reason behind each drop off, which is the rich detail the team needs at this stage. A rating survey gives a number without the story, server logs show where people stopped but not why, and a staff bench test does not capture real customer frustration. Use interviews first to find the causes, then survey a larger group to size them.
  81. The correct answer is A (Attractive, because guests are delighted when it is present and do not miss it): An attractive quality element creates satisfaction when it is present, but its absence causes no dissatisfaction, which matches what the guests described. A must-be element does the opposite and is noticed only when it is missing, a one-dimensional element lifts satisfaction in proportion to how much is delivered, and an indifferent element draws no reaction either way. Attractive features are useful differentiators, though they can fade into expectations over time.
  82. The correct answer is B (Break on time into the percent of parcels delivered by the promised hour): A critical to quality tree translates a general customer need into specific, measurable requirements, so on time becomes the percent of parcels delivered by the promised hour. Once the measure is defined, the team can collect a baseline and set a goal. Repeating the wish, buying more drivers, or copying the raw words into the charter leaves the requirement too vague to measure or improve.
  83. The correct answer is C (It states a cause before the data confirm one and points at a solution): A problem statement describes the gap between current and desired performance, with the size, the location, and the time frame, and it stays neutral about cause. Naming a vendor as the cause and the claims team as the solution owner commits the project before any data are collected and can send the team down the wrong path. The defect rate can be added as a measure, but the deeper fault is the premature cause and the built in solution.
  84. The correct answer is A (A SIPOC diagram that maps the process at a high level): SIPOC stands for suppliers, inputs, process, outputs, and customers, which is exactly the one page boundary view the team asked for. It is built before the detailed flowchart so the team agrees on where the process starts and stops and who it serves. A detailed flowchart or a value stream map adds step and time detail that comes later, and a work breakdown structure lists project tasks rather than process elements.
  85. The correct answer is D (An affinity diagram that sorts the notes into natural groupings): An affinity diagram is the tool for moving a large pile of unorganized ideas or comments into groups that share a theme, after which the team gives each group a short name. It is used early to bring order to messy qualitative input. A prioritization matrix ranks options against criteria, a tree diagram breaks one problem into levels, and a process decision program chart maps risks in a plan.
  86. The correct answer is B (About four days, because 120 claims divided by 30 per day is four): Little's Law says the average number of items in the process equals the completion rate times the average time in the process, so time equals work in process divided by throughput. Here 120 claims divided by 30 claims per day equals 4 days. Subtracting or adding the two numbers gives 90 or 150, which are not measures of flow time, and half a day confuses the daily completion rate with the waiting time.
  87. The correct answer is C (Stratified sampling, drawing a set number from each branch's own list): Stratified sampling divides the population into groups that differ from one another, then draws samples from each group, so every branch is represented in proportion to its share of the files. A simple random or systematic draw from one combined list can leave a small branch with few or no files. Convenience sampling is not representative because it depends on which files happen to arrive that morning.
  88. The correct answer is D (The process runs slightly low, since most of the misses fall below the target): Adding the histogram bars below 248.0 gives 3 plus 12, which is 15 bottles, and the bars above 252.0 give 3, so 18 of 180 fills, or 10 percent, land outside the limits. The misses are five times heavier on the low side, which points to a center that sits below the 250.0 target. The process is not centered, it does not meet the limits, and the spread is not the only issue because the two tails are unequal.
  89. The correct answer is A (The gage repeats well, but the appraisers apply the procedure differently): Repeatability is the variation when one person measures the same part again, and it is small here. Reproducibility is the variation between appraisers, and a large value means the two people are using the gage or the definition differently, often because the procedure is unclear. A worn gage would show up as poor repeatability, and part selection affects the study but does not explain a gap between appraisers.
  90. The correct answer is B (The median, because one extreme value pulls the mean upward): With nine calls near 5 minutes and one call at 45 minutes, the mean is pulled up by the outlier and no longer describes a typical call, while the median sits in the middle of the ordered values and stays near 5 minutes. The range and the standard deviation measure spread rather than the center. The mean is useful when the data are symmetric, but this set is not.
  91. The correct answer is C (The night shift runs slower and higher overall, with one unusually long wait): The night shift median is 10 minutes against 8 on days, its upper quartile and high are both larger, and the point at 26 minutes sits beyond the whisker as an outlier. Taken together, the night shift waits run longer and show more spread. The medians are not the same, the day shift is the tighter of the two, and nothing in the plot shows order volumes.
  92. The correct answer is D (The spread could fit the limits, but the process center is off target): Cp compares the specification width with the process spread and ignores the center, so a Cp of 1.60 says the spread is narrow enough. Cpk also accounts for how far the mean sits from the nearer limit, and its much lower value of 0.95 shows the process is off center. The two indices disagree, so the fix is to move the mean toward the target rather than to widen the limits.
  93. The correct answer is A (0.11, because 55 total defects divided by 500 units is 0.11): Start by counting all defects, not just the defective units. The 15 units with two defects contribute 30 defects and the 25 units with one defect contribute 25, giving 55 defects in total. Defects per unit is 55 divided by 500, which equals 0.11. The 0.08 figure counts units rather than defects, and the other two options divide in the wrong direction.
  94. The correct answer is B (The two variables move together, but a third factor may explain both): A correlation of 0.85 means the two measures rise and fall together across the 12 months, but correlation by itself cannot prove that one causes the other. A lurking variable such as hot summer weather can drive both ice cream sales and swimming, which lifts both numbers at the same time. The pattern is valid as a relationship, and it does not show that the variables are unrelated.
  95. The correct answer is D (A type II error, because a real effect was missed when the null was kept): A type II error, also called beta, happens when the test fails to reject the null even though the alternative is true, so a real improvement is missed. A type I error is the opposite, rejecting a true null and claiming an effect that is not there. Missing the seal improvement costs the plant the savings, so the team would want more power, often by increasing the sample size.

The largest single cause is 120 divided by 300, or 40 percent, not more than half, and the smallest cause is the last place to spend effort.

  99. The correct answer is C (At least one machine mean differs from the others by a real amount): Since the p value of 0.02 is smaller than the alpha of 0.05, the null hypothesis that all three means are equal is rejected, so at least one mean differs. ANOVA does not say which machine is different or how many are different, so the team must run a follow up comparison such as a pairwise test. Equal means are not supported, and the result is not inconclusive.
  100.   101.   102. The correct answer is A (Temperature is a factor, and hardness is the response variable): In a design of experiments, the factors are the inputs the team sets or changes, such as temperature and bake time, and the response is the output that is measured, which here is cookie hardness. Factor levels are the specific settings, so two temperature settings and three time settings give six treatment combinations in a full factorial. Hardness is not a factor or a block, and temperature is not a level.
  103. Waiting refers to idle time, over-processing refers to doing more work than the customer needs, and inventory refers to material or goods held between steps.

  106.   107. Adding capacity anywhere else, including a second assembly line, will not raise output because the constraint has not moved. Deliberately slowing a non constraint lowers throughput, and shifting people away from the constraint can make it worse.

  109.   110. The correct answer is A (It adds variation, because adjusting a stable process increases the spread): A point inside the control limits on a stable chart comes from common cause variation, which belongs to the system and will vary on its own. Reacting to it by adjusting the process is called tampering, and Deming showed through the funnel experiment that tampering adds variation instead of removing it. The point is not evidence of a problem, adjusting it does not improve stability, and the adjustment is far more likely to raise variability than to move the mean usefully.
  111. The correct answer is C (The pattern shows a shift, so the team should look for a special cause): A run chart is read by looking at runs of points on one side of the center line. Eight points in a row below the line followed by eight above it is far more than chance would produce, which signals that the process has shifted, so the team should search for a special cause at the point of change. Staying near the center line is not enough to claim stability, and the final week is part of the pattern rather than the cause.
  112.   113. The correct answer is B (Control limits, not specification limits, tell whether the process has changed): Control limits come from the variation in the process itself and are used to detect special causes, while specification limits come from the customer and define what is acceptable. Mixing them confuses two different questions, one about stability and one about conformance. Drawing both on one chart can mislead operators into reacting to common cause points, and specification limits never replace control limits.
  114. Options A, B, and D all treat one successful site as proof that the change will work everywhere.
  159. The correct answer is D (Inventory, motion, and overprocessing, in the order the findings appear.): Finished pumps waiting in a rack are inventory because the material sits idle, operators walking to a far tool crib are motion, and clerks retyping data the scanner already captured is overprocessing, since the second entry adds no value. Options A, B, and C each misclassify at least one of the three findings, so they do not match the order in which the wastes appear.
  160. The correct answer is B (The single head plan, because each subgroup stays inside one set of conditions.): Rational subgrouping groups units so variation inside a subgroup comes from common causes and differences between subgroups can reveal the change the team wants to catch. Five consecutive bottles from one head share a short period, one machine, and one set of conditions. Mixing six heads into one subgroup hides any difference between the heads, which defeats the purpose of the chart, so options A, C, and D work against the principle. Bottle to bottle differences inside one head's hourly subgroup are common cause variation, and a special cause is what appears as a shift from one subgroup to the next, so option D states the idea backward.
  161. The correct answer is A (Look for a special cause at subgroup 9 and fix it before recalculating limits.): A point beyond a control limit is a signal that something outside the usual system acted on the process, so the first step is to find and remove that special cause. Only after the cause is understood and corrected does the team consider whether the limits should be recomputed. Option B skips the investigation, option C hides the signal, and option D adjusts a process when a single point is the issue.
  162. The correct answer is C (A p chart, which plots a proportion and allows the sample size to vary.): Attribute data come in two flavors, and the choice of chart follows from whether the team counts nonconforming units or nonconformities and whether the sample size is fixed. A proportion with a changing sample size calls for a p chart, which recalculates the limits for each sample. An np chart needs a constant sample size, a c chart counts defects in a fixed area, and an X bar and R chart needs measured data.
  163. The correct answer is B (Repeat the measurement study, because the gauge now supplies 45 percent of the spread.): Gauge share is gauge variation divided by total process variation. Before the improvement the share was 0.09 divided by 0.40, which is 22.5 percent, an acceptable figure under the usual guideline. After the improvement the share is 0.09 divided by 0.20, which is 45 percent, above the 30 percent guideline, so the gauge is now the largest single source of variation and real signals hide. Options A, C, and D either keep a gauge that is no longer adequate or change the wrong thing.
  164. The correct answer is D (The control plan, which names the owner, the checks, and the reactions.): The control plan is the written summary of how the improved process is kept on track, and it is the formal handoff document because it names the process owner, the controlled characteristics, the sample sizes and frequencies, and the reaction when a signal appears. The lessons learned file records history, the charter states the original problem, and the pilot report describes an early test, so none of them carries the running controls.
  165. The correct answer is A (DMADOV, which adds an optimize phase between design and verify.): Both roadmaps start with define, measure, and analyze, and both end with verify. DMADOV inserts an optimize step after design, where the team tunes the design against several responses before verification confirms it. DMADV moves from design straight to verify, DMAIC improves an existing process, and PDCA is a general improvement cycle, so only DMADOV places the tuning step where the team described it.
  166. The correct answer is C (Design for maintainability, so the bed can be serviced and cleaned quickly.): Design for X covers the constraints a design must satisfy, and design for maintainability reduces the time and effort needed to service, clean, and repair the product. A bed that takes two staff members thirty minutes to strip for cleaning has a maintainability problem. Design for test adds diagnostic points, design for cost targets price, and design for producibility simplifies how the item is built, so none of the other three fits the cleaning delay.
  167. The correct answer is B (Score each idea against strategy, customer impact, cost of poor quality, and measurable gain): Project screening in the body of knowledge weighs strategy fit, customer impact, the cost of poor quality, and measurable return. That logic keeps scarce Black Belt time on work with a real payoff. The other options pick projects by speed, personal interest, or headcount. None of those predicts a business result.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Combine them under one system with shared project selection and shared reviews): Six Sigma, lean, and problem solving all work better under one deployment system with one project list and one review cycle. Separate programs can bend the same resources and count the same savings more than once. Cancelling the other methods throws away tools the business still needs. Waiting for an audit lets duplicate claims grow before anyone resolves them.
  2. The correct answer is A (Build a balanced scorecard that links each goal to measures, targets, owners, and review dates): A balanced scorecard ties financial, customer, process, and learning measures to the strategy. It also names a target and an owner for each measure. Watching one financial number hides the drivers of the result. Department measures chosen on their own can conflict, so the scorecard keeps the organization pointed at shared goals.
  3. The correct answer is A (A value proposition that names the benefits offered and the customers who receive them): A value proposition states what the organization gives customers, which customers it serves, and why that offer beats the alternatives. Mission and values describe purpose and conduct. A cost target is an internal goal rather than a customer promise. A complaint list is a source of project ideas, not a statement of value.
  4. The correct answer is B (Value added time is five minutes, a small share of total lead time): Value added work is 2 minutes plus 3 minutes, which is 5 minutes. Total lead time is 5 plus 6 plus 9, which is 20 minutes, so the value added share is 5 divided by 20, or 25 percent. Waiting is the larger part of the process. Option A counts the fifteen minutes of waiting as value added work, and option D reverses the ratio by treating the 25 percent value added share as 75 percent efficiency.
  5. The correct answer is A (A single owner for each measure, plus a target and a review cycle that drives action): Process measures change behavior when each one has a named owner, a target, and a regular review where the owner explains results. A bigger dashboard adds noise rather than action. Repeating numbers by email spreads information without assigning responsibility. Ranking people by movement in a measure can also push them to manage the number rather than the process.
  6. The correct answer is B (Use the comparison to find a gap, then study the practices behind the best rates): Benchmarking shows where a gap exists and points the team toward organizations that perform better. The next step is to learn how the better performers achieve their results. Copying another site without study can fail because processes and patients differ. A single comparison cannot prove the process is unsafe, and waiting a year wastes the chance to improve now.
  7. The correct answer is A (Standardize the defect definition and the data collection method before adding the counts): Measures of the same idea must share one definition and one collection method before anyone adds them. Different definitions make the totals meaningless because each area counts a different thing. Reporting them separately hides the total cost of poor quality. Ranking areas by budget picks a target without evidence about where defects come from.
  8. The correct answer is A (Include the doubter, because informed challenge improves decisions and builds later support): A skilled skeptic who knows the process can surface risks and assumptions the team would otherwise miss. People who help shape a change also tend to support it later. Leaving the person out removes the challenge but keeps the doubt alive in the wider workforce. Delaying concerns removes their value, and a quarterly side review has no influence on daily decisions.
  9. The correct answer is A (A roles and responsibilities list that states each member's duties and decision rights): A roles and responsibilities list tells each member what to do and what they may decide without asking. That prevents gaps and double work. Training everyone in every job may help understanding but does not settle ownership. Seniority does not make a person the right facilitator, and a budget split is a cost matter rather than a team design tool.
  10. The correct answer is C (Ask both members to bring evidence and test each claim against the process data): Data resolves disagreements that opinion cannot. Asking each member to bring evidence also keeps both people in the discussion as contributors. Seniority is not evidence about a machine adjustment. Sending the issue to the sponsor delays a decision the team can make with data, and leaving the members alone leaves the question open.
  11. The correct answer is B (Re-plan the meetings with the sponsor and confirm that members are truly released): Missed tollgates often mean members were never really freed from daily work. The fix starts with the sponsor resetting priorities and the meeting schedule. Replacing people loses process knowledge and may repeat the same problem. Dropping reviews removes the checkpoints that keep the project honest, and adding meetings does not create time.
  12. The correct answer is D (Agree on the decision criteria and their weights before comparing the two options): When experts disagree about results, the team usually lacks agreed decision criteria. Setting the criteria and their weights first turns a debate about opinions into a comparison against shared standards. Seniority does not settle which outcome matters more. Running both options for a year delays care improvement, and choosing on cost alone ignores the performance question.
  13. The correct answer is C (Rewrite the problem statement with a baseline, a target, a scope, and a time frame): A charter must state where the process begins and ends, how it performs today, what target the team will reach, and by when. Without those parts the team cannot tell whether the project succeeded. Approving it as written leaves direction without a measure. Separate departmental charters split the problem, and a complaint list is raw input rather than a charter.
  14. The correct answer is A (A customer segmentation review that lists each group's needs, importance, and priority): Customer segmentation separates the different groups, states what each one needs, and shows which needs carry the most weight. That lets the team set a clear scope instead of trying to satisfy every request. Delivery time data describes current performance. A financial statement ranks internal budgets, and a training matrix records skills rather than customer needs.
  15. The correct answer is D (Turn the request into a measurable goal such as cutting check in time to six minutes): A general wish becomes usable only when it turns into a measurable goal with a baseline and a target. That lets the team measure progress and confirm that customers feel the change. Letting each hotel choose its own fix prevents a shared result. Lobby design is one possible cause rather than the goal, and telling staff to work faster picks a solution before the data is studied.
  16. The correct answer is B (A responsibility matrix that names the person accountable, consulted, and informed for each task): A responsibility matrix settles the roles the question asks about. It names the accountable person, the people to consult, and the people to keep informed for each task. A gantt chart shows timing rather than authority. A budget tracker covers money and a risk log covers threats, so neither states who may approve a change or accept the deliverable.
  17. The correct answer is D (Define trust as measures such as error rate, delivery by a set date, and format clarity): A vague voice of the customer comment becomes usable when the team names the measurable parts behind it. Error rate, delivery timing, and format clarity give the project a requirement to test. Dropping the comment ignores a real need. Repeating the survey adds more vague words, and asking only about cost skips what the customer actually wants.
  18. The correct answer is C (State a measure, a baseline, a target, and a deadline for the improvement): A usable goal names the measure, where it stands today, the level to reach, and the date to reach it. That makes success verifiable at the close. A vague promise cannot be judged. A zero defect goal is often unreachable and discourages honest reporting, and a goal that can be changed at any time provides no standard.
  19. The correct answer is A (A quality function deployment matrix that links customer wants to design features): Quality function deployment maps each customer want to the design features that influence it, and it weights the wants by importance. That shows which features earn a place in the design. A control chart watches stability and a pareto chart ranks one variable at a time. A scatter diagram shows the link between two features, so none of them connects wants to features across the whole design.
  20. The correct answer is C (Check that supplier's setup and process steps before accepting the next shipment): A value plotted above the upper fence signals a special cause such as a setup error or a mixed lot. The team should find the reason before accepting more parts. Averaging hides the outlier, and ignoring it removes evidence without finding a cause. Raising the specification changes the design requirement to fit the part instead of improving the supplier process.
  21. The correct answer is D (Improve the measurement system first, before trusting its readings for capability work): Measurement variation near 30 percent of the total is large against the common rule of thumb. When the gauge consumes that much variation, the team cannot separate process change from measurement noise. Waiting a year leaves the risk in place. Swapping the gauge before finding whether the cause is the gauge, the part, or the operator skips the diagnostic step.
  22. The correct answer is D (The standard table applies, and the report should state the one and a half sigma shift): Defects per million opportunities is converted to a sigma level with a standard table. The short term figure includes the one and a half sigma shift convention, and naming that convention keeps the report honest. Dividing defects by units gives a rate rather than a sigma level, and multiplying by yield has no basis. Specification limits are not needed for this conversion.
  23. The correct answer is B (The Poisson distribution, which models counts of events in a fixed interval): The Poisson distribution models the number of events in a fixed interval when those events arrive independently at a stable rate. Calls per minute fit that setting. The normal distribution describes a measured value, and the binomial distribution counts successes in a set number of trials. The exponential distribution describes the waiting time between arrivals rather than the count.
  24. The correct answer is B (The pooled standard deviation, which combines the variation of both groups): The pooled standard deviation combines the within group variation of both samples and weights each by its degrees of freedom. Weighting matters because the two samples may not be the same size. Averaging the two standard deviations ignores sample size. Pooling all twenty parts mixes in between group differences, and choosing the larger value is not an estimate at all.
  25. The correct answer is C (The readings are biased, so the team must correct them before judging the process center): A reading that runs one percent low on every part is a bias, which means the measurement misses the true value in the same direction each time. Bias shifts the average, so averaging more parts will not remove it. A steady offset does not cancel itself. A correction or known adjustment is needed before the process center can be judged.
  26. The correct answer is B (One lot produced an unusual reading, and the variation across the lots needs attention): Four readings sit between 0.42 and 0.45 while one sits at 0.62, which is far outside the run of the others. That points to a special cause in one lot or a measurement problem worth checking. The first four readings do not show a center shift, and four close readings out of five do not establish stability. Nothing in the table proves the gauge is broken.
  27. The correct answer is C (The sample must be drawn so every order in the week has a chance): A probability sample gives every order in the week a known chance of being picked, which is what makes the result representative. Standing at the dock on Monday morning takes a convenience sample from one place at one time, so it carries whatever pattern that shift and day hold. Leaving the pick to whoever appears first does not make the draw random. Keeping a log records the sample but does not repair how the orders were selected.
  28. The correct answer is A (Sample means approach a normal distribution as the sample size grows, whatever the shape): The central limit theorem states that the distribution of sample means moves toward normal as the sample size increases, even when the underlying data are skewed. The skew of the individual values does not carry into the means in the same way. The result does not require normal original data. Thirty is a common rule of thumb rather than a requirement.
  29. The correct answer is A (The link shows association, so a controlled study is needed before claiming that training drives sales): A correlation shows that two measures move together, not that one causes the other. A controlled study such as a pilot with matched stores can test the causal claim. Saying the link proves cause goes too far. Saying it is meaningless ignores a real pattern, and claiming that sales cause training is a different guess with no more support.
  30. The correct answer is B (The difference is significant, so the team rejects the null hypothesis at this alpha): When the p value falls below the chosen alpha, the result is statistically significant and the null hypothesis is rejected. Here 0.03 is below 0.05, so the team rejects the null. The rule does not depend on a sample size of thirty. A repeat test is useful confirmation rather than a condition for significance.
  31. The correct answer is C (The coefficient of determination, which is about 0.86 for this data set): The coefficient of determination is the square of the correlation. Squaring minus 0.93 gives 0.8649, so line speed explains about 86 percent of the variation in fill weight. The correlation coefficient measures the strength and direction of a linear link but is not a share of variation. The slope gives change per unit, and the standard error measures scatter around the line.
  32. The correct answer is C (It ranks this mode against others, and the team should start with the highest scores): The risk priority number is severity times occurrence times detection, so 8 times 6 times 4 equals 192. It is a ranking aid that helps the team compare failure modes and begin with the highest values. It does not predict that a failure will happen. It is not a fixed threshold from any standard, and a high severity still matters.
  33. The correct answer is D (Study the top three reasons and fix the causes behind those bars first): A pareto chart orders causes by size, and the top few often account for most of the problem. Focusing there returns the largest gain for the time spent. Working across every bar at once dilutes the effort. Starting with the smallest bar delays the payoff, and repeating the study without acting leaves the rejections in place.
  34. The correct answer is C (Collect data on the frequency of each cause and display it in a pareto chart): A pareto chart turns frequency data into a ranking, so the team can see which causes carry the largest share and act there first. A fishbone diagram organizes possible causes but does not weigh them. One person's ranking is an opinion. Running many tests on one data set raises the chance of a false finding.
  35. The correct answer is C (Fifty six minutes, found by multiplying 2.5 by 20 and adding the intercept of 6): The fitted line says predicted time equals the intercept plus the slope times distance. Multiplying 2.5 minutes per mile by 20 miles gives 50 minutes. Adding the intercept of 6 minutes gives 56 minutes. Ignoring the intercept drops the baseline time, and the other options misuse the formula.
  36. The correct answer is D (Eight runs, because two levels raised to the third power equals eight combinations): A full factorial with three factors at two levels has two raised to the third power combinations, and 2 times 2 times 2 equals 8. Each combination appears once before any repeat. Six runs would leave combinations untested. Nine adds center points that are not part of the minimum design, and twelve multiplies factors in a way the design does not require.
  37. The correct answer is A (About 59 percent, found by multiplying availability, performance, and quality together): Availability is the 400 minutes of run time after the 80 lost minutes divided by the 480 scheduled minutes, which is 0.833. Performance compares the 900 parts made with the 1,200 parts the ideal rate of three parts per minute allows in the 400 minutes of run time, which is 0.75. Quality is 846 good parts divided by 900, or 0.94. Multiplying 0.833 by 0.75 by 0.94 gives about 0.59, so the figure is about 59 percent.
  38. The correct answer is B (The waiting and recheck steps, because both consume time without adding value): Waiting and rework are classic wastes of time and effort. The team can attack them without touching the approval rules that define the process. Changing the signature rule alters policy. Travel is one part of the waste rather than the only part, and blaming the clerk targets a person instead of a process step.
  39. The correct answer is C (Temperature has the strong effect, so the next step is to confirm and optimize that setting): The temperature effect of about twelve points is large next to the pressure effect of under one point. Temperature deserves the next round of study to find its best setting. Dropping pressure goes beyond this data, since it may matter at other levels. A valid design can show that one factor does little, so the experiment did not fail.
  40. The correct answer is C (Twenty four containers, found by multiplying demand by lead time, then dividing by container size): The kanban count equals daily demand times replenishment lead time divided by the container size. So 300 units per day times 4 days equals 1,200 units, and 1,200 divided by 50 units per container equals 24 containers before any safety allowance. Dividing by container size alone ignores lead time. The other options combine the numbers in ways the formula does not use.
  41. The correct answer is D (The setup reduction method that separates internal work from external work): Setup reduction divides changeover tasks into internal work that needs the machine stopped and external work that can happen while it runs. Moving preheating and staging to the running period is exactly that step. The five why method finds root causes and kanban sizes containers. Poka yoke prevents mistakes, so none of those describes the changeover work.
  42. The correct answer is D (The bottleneck has moved, and the team must focus the next improvement on the new limit): When the constraint at the filler is lifted, another step becomes the limit, which is why total output hardly changed. The theory of constraints says to find the new bottleneck and improve there. Speeding every step wastes effort because only the constraint holds the line back. A buffer does not fix a slow labeler, and undoing the gain helps no one.
  43. The correct answer is A (A control plan that names each measure, chart, sample plan, owner, and reaction step): A control plan states what is monitored, how often, who owns it, and what to do when a signal appears. That is what keeps a gain from slipping away. A final report records history rather than daily control. A video of the old method can confuse operators, and a charter describes the original problem rather than the new standard.
  44. The correct answer is B (Keep monitoring with the check sheet, because a gain can erode once attention moves): Improvements drift back when routine monitoring stops and people return to old habits. Three weeks is a short window, and it does not cover changes in material, staffing, or demand. Waiting for a customer complaint means the defect has already escaped. A yearly review is far too slow to catch a shift in the process.
  45. The correct answer is C (Assign chart review to a named owner and rehearse the reaction plan with the team): An unread chart cannot control anything. The fix is to make someone accountable for reviewing it and to practice the response before a real signal appears. Tighter limits would raise false alarms. Repeating the whole project wastes work already done, and a monthly report is slower than the chart it replaces.
  46. The correct answer is D (A reaction plan that lists the checks, the escalation path, and the decision owner): A reaction plan tells the operator and supervisor what to check, whom to call, and who decides the next action. Without it, people either overreact or wait for someone else. No process stays fixed after a project. A data archive does not guide action, and treating the chart as display removes the control the project put in place.
  47. The correct answer is D (A signal is present, so the team should investigate what changed around subgroup 7): A point beyond a control limit is a signal that a special cause is likely present. Control limits come from the process itself rather than from the specifications. The right response is to look for what changed at that time. Capability is a separate question, widening specifications changes the design requirement, and a single crossing does not prove the limits are wrong.
  48. The correct answer is B (Use DMADV, because the product is new and the process must be designed rather than repaired): DMADV is the design method for a new product or process. The team defines requirements, measures what customers need, analyzes design options, designs the solution, and verifies it. DMAIC improves an existing process and starts from a current baseline. A new platform has no baseline process to improve, so control cannot come first and DMAIC is the wrong starting point.
  49. The correct answer is B (The dimensions vary independently and follow known distributions, so extremes rarely line up): Statistical tolerancing assumes the part dimensions vary independently and follow known distributions. That makes the chance of every part sitting at its worst limit very small. The stack can then be estimated with a root sum square style calculation. Assuming dimensions sit at the limits describes the worst case method, and assuming one shared direction would overstate the stack.
  50. The correct answer is A (The champion, who clears obstacles and secures resources for project teams.): The champion is the senior sponsor who removes barriers, finds resources, and keeps the project aligned with the business goals. A project stalls when a department withholds the people it needs, and the champion holds the authority to resolve that conflict. The financial analyst confirms savings but does not control staffing. The green belt collects data and the process owner runs the improved process, and neither role can direct another department to release staff.
  51. The correct answer is B (Identify the stakeholders who are affected and assess their readiness.): Change management begins with stakeholder analysis and a readiness assessment, because the plan has to answer the concerns of the people who will live with the new process. Publishing a diagram, setting a date, or rewarding early adopters all come after the team knows who is affected and how ready each group is. Starting with the announcement would leave the objections from the two technical leads unaddressed.
  52. The correct answer is C (The expected financial benefit and the link to strategic goals.): Screening criteria compare ideas on expected benefit, strategic fit, risk, and effort, and the benefit and the link to the business goals come first because they decide whether an idea deserves resources at all. The number of available belts affects scheduling. The department that suggested the idea and the statistical difficulty of the work are secondary factors rather than the primary screen.
  53. The correct answer is D (Contingency planning, which prepares backup actions for identified risks.): Contingency planning names the risks that could derail the program and the backup action for each one, so a supplier shutdown has a prepared response. A Gantt chart and a work breakdown structure organize the work but do not prepare for a risk. A toll-gate review checks progress at set points and does not create a fallback plan.
  54. The correct answer is B (Competitive benchmarking, because the comparison targets direct rivals.): Competitive benchmarking compares performance and methods against direct competitors in the same market, which is what the manager plans to do. Collaborative benchmarking involves sharing data with another organization, often from outside the market. Best-practice benchmarking hunts for a proven method wherever it exists, and breakthrough benchmarking aims at a large leap rather than a like-for-like comparison.
  55. The correct answer is C (It is a lagging indicator, so it reports results after the service is delivered.): Complaints are counted after customers have lived through the service, so the number describes performance that has already happened. A leading indicator measures something that predicts future complaints, such as unresolved cases sitting in the queue. A behavior indicator tracks actions that staff take, and the complaint rate is an outcome rather than a financial measure.
  56. The correct answer is A (Scrap savings are a hard benefit, and fewer complaints are a soft benefit.): Hard benefits appear in the accounts as real cash, and the finance team confirmed the scrap figure from the profit and loss statement. Fewer complaints are a soft benefit because the gain is expected rather than yet recorded in the accounts. Calling both hard, calling both soft, or swapping the two labels misreads how the finance function classifies project gains.
  57. The correct answer is D (Faster cycles can strain suppliers, so their capacity deserves a review.): A shorter internal cycle pushes demand onto suppliers more quickly, so their capacity and lead times become part of the redesigned process. Removing them from scope ignores that they are stakeholders in the change. Higher volumes do not automatically offset strain, and halting the project until every supplier agrees asks for more control than the risk requires.
  58. The correct answer is D (Storming, where disagreements surface before the team settles into a rhythm.): Storming is the stage where members test ideas, roles, and boundaries, and open disagreement about methods is typical. Forming is the polite introduction stage, norming is where roles and rules settle, and adjourning comes when the work is finished. Naming the stage helps the Black Belt normalize the conflict and guide the team toward its working rhythm.
  59. The correct answer is B (Send the agenda in advance and start each meeting at the posted time.): Meetings lose their decisions when members arrive without the topics and the start time keeps sliding, so an advance agenda and a firm start time protect the team's time. A longer meeting adds more of the same problem, asking late members to present first rewards the lateness, and moving the day may not suit the wider group.
  60. The correct answer is C (Nominal group technique, which gathers written ideas before the group discusses them.): Nominal group technique has each member write ideas or ratings before any discussion, so the quiet members' views are captured before the vocal members can sway the room. Majority voting still lets the largest bloc decide, consensus asks for agreement without equalizing input, and an open round repeats the very dynamic that produced the problem.
  61. The correct answer is A (Whether the person brings needed expertise and can influence the pharmacy staff.): Team member selection weighs relevant skills, subject knowledge, the ability to influence the process, and availability, and the nurse manager can bring clinical insight that the pharmacy team lacks. Calendar space and past work with the Black Belt are helpful but secondary. A senior title by itself does not supply the expertise the project needs.
  62. The correct answer is B (Turn the identified skill gaps into learning objectives for the sessions.): A training needs assessment starts with the skills gap and converts it into learning objectives, which then shape the plan, the materials, and the delivery. Choosing a vendor and scheduling sessions come later. Delivering the training before the objectives exist would leave the content and the evaluation unplanned.
  63. The correct answer is D (Each segment values different features, so the requirements will differ.): Customer segmentation separates groups with different needs, so travel agents, corporate clients, and leisure guests each describe value in their own way. Letting the largest group set every requirement would miss the others. Paying guests are not the only source of valid customer data, and travel agents shape a large share of bookings even though they do not stay in the hotel.
  64. The correct answer is A (Clean tables are a must-be need, and fast mobile ordering is an attractive need.): A must-be need is expected as a basic standard, so a dirty table causes dissatisfaction while a clean one earns no praise. An attractive need delights customers when it is present and causes no complaint when it is missing, which fits the surprise at fast mobile ordering. The one-dimensional and indifferent labels describe different patterns of customer satisfaction.
  65. The correct answer is C (Cut billing errors from 4.2 percent to 1.0 percent by March 31.): A SMART goal states the measure, the starting point, the target, and the date, so the team can tell later whether it succeeded. The other drafts name quality in general terms, ask for the lowest level the team can reach, or describe a study rather than a result, so none of them can be measured against a baseline.
  66. The correct answer is D (Adult patients at the central campus, from triage through bed assignment.): The scope section limits the project to one campus and to the adult emergency journey that the goal describes. Ambulance arrivals at every hospital widen the boundaries well beyond the charter. Pediatric services and urgent care clinics appear as out of scope, so those options describe work that this project does not cover.
  67. The correct answer is B (An affinity diagram, which sorts the ideas into related groups.): An affinity diagram gathers many overlapping ideas and sorts them into themes, which is exactly what the facilitator wants before the team chooses a cause to study. A tree diagram breaks a goal into levels, a prioritization matrix scores options, and an activity network diagram sequences tasks, so none of them performs the grouping step.
  68. The correct answer is A (A RACI matrix, which maps each task to its four role holders.): The four questions in the scenario match the four RACI roles, responsible, accountable, consulted, and informed. A Gantt chart places tasks in time, a work breakdown structure divides deliverables into levels, and a toll-gate review approves the project at set stages, so none of them assigns those four roles.
  69. The correct answer is C (Shift is nominal, and package weight is measured on a ratio scale.): A nominal scale labels categories with no order, which fits day, evening, and night. A ratio scale has a true zero and allows meaningful ratios, which fits a weight measured in grams. Calling the shift labels ordinal would claim an order that the labels do not carry, and treating weight as nominal would ignore its numeric meaning.
  70. The correct answer is D (The first result is repeatability, and the second is reproducibility.): Repeatability is the variation seen when one operator measures the same parts again under the same conditions, which matches the first result. Reproducibility is the variation between operators, which matches the differing averages. Bias describes a consistent offset from a reference, and linearity describes how that offset changes across the measuring range.
  71. The correct answer is B (The process is roughly centered but produces about 8.5 percent outside the limits.): The histogram is centered near the target with a bell shape, so the process is not off center. Dividing 17 readings by 200 gives 0.085, which is 8.5 percent outside the limits and too high for a filling operation. Widening the limits or declaring the process capable would accept the loss rather than reduce it.
  72. The correct answer is A (Cp ignores where the process is centered, and Cpk accounts for that shift.): Cp compares the specification width with the process spread, so it does not change when the mean moves away from the center. Cpk measures the distance from the mean to the nearer specification limit, so a shifted process scores lower. The mean sits three standard deviations above the lower limit, so that side gives a value of 1.00 and the index cannot exceed it. Short-term and long-term data separate Cp from Pp, and the subgroup range is used for control limits rather than for this index.
  73. The correct answer is C (0.849, the product of the four first pass yields.): Rolled throughput yield multiplies the first pass yields of the steps. Here 0.98 times 0.97 equals 0.9506, times 0.95 equals 0.90307, and times 0.94 equals 0.84888, which rounds to 0.849. Averaging the four yields gives 0.960 and describes a process that does not exist. Taking the lowest yield ignores the losses at the other steps, and stopping after three steps leaves out the fourth.

Day and swing do not share a median, and the swing median of 10 minutes is not the shortest of the three. A box plot shows the distribution of the data and does not prove what caused the longer waits.
  75. The correct answer is A (Stratified sampling, which draws a set number from each regional office.): Error rates differ by office, so the sample needs a known number of invoices from each one to represent the file fairly. Systematic sampling by an arbitrary rule such as even numbers does not guarantee coverage, and convenience sampling lets the easiest invoices bias the estimate.

  77. The correct answer is C (A type II error, which a larger sample would have made less likely.): A type II error means the test failed to detect a difference that really exists, and its probability falls when the sample grows or the test gains power. A type I error is the reverse case, where a test finds a difference that is not real. Equipment failure and a correct conclusion of no difference do not describe the outcome.
  78. A few points off the line do not remove the pattern, and the direction is positive rather than negative.
  79. The correct answer is A (A chi square test of independence for the two categorical variables.): Both variables are categories, branch and error type, so a chi square test of independence checks whether the counts in the table depart from what independence would predict. The t tests and the analysis of variance compare means of a measured response, which the table of counts by category does not provide.
  80.   81. A chi square test and a test of proportions work with counts and rates rather than measured strengths.

  86. The correct answer is A (Blocking, which groups runs by day so the day effect is separated.): Blocking places runs that share a nuisance factor into groups so that factor does not contaminate the comparison of interest, and the day of the week fits that role here. Confounding merges an effect with another on purpose, which is the opposite of what the team wants.
