The correct answer is B (The chart will show false signals and prompt tampering with a stable process): Control limits come from the natural variation of the process, so narrowing them creates signals that do not reflect real change. Reacting to those false signals is tampering, and tampering with a stable process tends to add variation rather than remove it. Missing real shifts belongs to limits that are too wide rather than too narrow. Option C confuses control limits with specification limits, and option D misunderstands how the center line is set.
  143. The correct answer is A (A p chart, because it tracks the fraction defective with varying sample size): A p chart plots the proportion defective and adjusts its limits for each sample size, which suits daily counts that range from 40 to 120 kits. An np chart tracks the number defective, so it needs a constant sample size. An X bar and R chart plots a measured value rather than a proportion, and a c chart counts defects in a constant unit rather than the fraction of defective items.
  144. The correct answer is C (Subgroup six is a special cause signal that the team should investigate now): A point beyond a control limit is a signal of a special cause, and subgroup six plots at 13.4 against an upper limit of 13.0. The team should look for what changed at that time, such as a material lot, a shift, or a setup. Option A mislabels the nine points inside the limits as the cause, option B treats one outside point as harmless, and option D proposes recalculating limits before the cause is understood.
  145. The correct answer is D (Eight points on one side is a run signal that shows a process shift): A run of eight points on one side of the center line is one of the standard signals, even when every point sits inside the control limits, because the pattern shows the process level has moved. The analyst is watching only the single point rule and missing the run rule. Option A repeats that error, option B would hide the shift by moving the center line, and option C would widen the limits to mask a real change.
  146. The correct answer is B (A control plan, which keeps the improved process on track after handover): A control plan lists each measure, the sample size and frequency, the chart or test, the reaction plan when a signal appears, and the owner, which is exactly the list in the question. A project charter authorizes the project at the start, a value stream map shows process steps and timing, and a Pareto chart ranks defects. Only the control plan carries the ongoing monitoring details into operations.
  147. The correct answer is A (Standardize the method, keep the checklist, and assign ongoing measure reviews): The gain faded because the revised method was not built into training, the checklist left the work packet, and nobody owned the measure, so the fix is to lock those three in place. Reopening the project and running more experiments adds cost without addressing the missing standard. Adding an inspection step detects defects after the fact, and raising the specification limits hides the problem instead of holding the gain.
  148. The correct answer is D (DMADV, which defines, measures, analyzes, designs, and verifies a new process): DFSS uses DMADV when a new process or product is being designed and there is no existing process to improve, which matches the request to define requirements, measure them, analyze design options, design, and verify. DMAIC improves an existing process, so it does not fit a process that does not yet exist. PDCA is a general improvement cycle for existing work, and DMADOV adds an optimization step that the described method does not include.
  149. The correct answer is C (Statistical tolerancing, which combines part variation to set a working limit): Statistical tolerancing uses the way individual part variations combine in an assembly, often by a root sum square method, to set limits that keep the product working without demanding the tightest possible dimensions. Design for test adds inspection points, poka yoke prevents assembly errors, and design for maintainability plans service and repair, so none of those addresses how tolerances stack up. Choosing tolerances this way balances cost against the variation the assembly can absorb.
  150. The correct answer is A (Link projects to strategic goals and rank them by expected business impact): Enterprise deployment works when projects trace back to the business strategy and are ranked by expected benefit. When teams pick their own topics, the savings arrive in scattered forms that leadership cannot compare or defend. Letting each department set its own targets keeps the effort fragmented instead of focused. Software and extra training do not repair a missing link between the projects and the strategy.
  151. The correct answer is B (The system is in the conditional band and should be judged by risk and cost): Divide 0.030 by 0.200 and the gauge consumes 15 percent of the tolerance. The common guideline treats under 10 percent as acceptable, 10 to 30 percent as conditional, and over 30 percent as unacceptable. At 15 percent the decision depends on the risk of a wrong call and the cost of improving the gauge. Option A is wrong because the value sits above 10 percent, and option C is wrong because it sits below 30 percent.
  152. The correct answer is C (The observed difference is unlikely if the lines truly had equal means): A p-value is the chance of seeing data at least as extreme as the sample result if the null hypothesis of equal means were true. Option A confuses that with the chance that the null itself is true. A small p-value says the difference is unlikely under the null, not that the difference matters in the plant, so option B overstates it. Alpha is set before the test, and raising it afterward to force a result breaks the logic of the test, so option D is wrong.
  153. The correct answer is D (It bundles several outcomes with no baseline, target, or single measure): A problem statement needs one focused outcome, a starting value, a target, and a way to measure progress. This charter names four unrelated outcomes and gives no baseline, so no one can prove whether the project worked. Option A is not the issue because no milestones are listed at all. Option B is not the issue because the missing sponsor is not the defect described, and option C is wrong because customer satisfaction is a valid customer measure.
  154. The correct answer is B (The percentage of orders picked on the first pass without a recheck): First pass pick accuracy is measured during the work, so a drop warns leaders before customers ever feel it. Complaints and late deliveries are lagging measures because they record harm that already happened. Option A counts complaints already closed, which is still backward looking. Option C is a financial total reported weeks later, and option D is another customer-reported outcome.
  155. The correct answer is A (Motion, because the walking itself consumes time without adding value): Walking to a distant supply room is movement of people that the patient does not pay for, which is the waste called motion. The supplies are consumed correctly and no work is repeated, so the problem is the travel rather than the material or the quality. Inventory waste in option B is stock sitting idle, and no evidence of stocked material is given. Waiting in option C is idle time for a person or item, not travel, and option D would require actual errors.
  156. The correct answer is C (Storming, because members are testing roles, influence, and how decisions get made): Storming is the stage where members jockey for influence and challenge how the group will work, which matches the quiet testing described. Forming in option A is polite orientation where the group is still learning its purpose, but here members have already begun testing roles and influence. Performing in option B requires settled roles and productive output. Adjourning in option D happens when the work ends, and this team has not started it.
  157. The correct answer is D (About 0.67, limited by how close the mean sits to the upper limit): The upper side is (25.10 minus 25.06) divided by (3 times 0.02), which is 0.04 divided by 0.06, or 0.67. The lower side is (25.06 minus 24.90) divided by 0.06, which is 0.16 divided by 0.06, or 2.67. Cpk takes the smaller of the two sides, so the answer is 0.67. Option A reports the larger one-sided value, and options B and C divide the one-sided distances by six sigma instead of three.
  158. The correct answer is A (The first mode, because its risk priority number of 240 is higher): The risk priority number multiplies severity, occurrence, and detection. Option B describes severity alone, which does not set the ranking even though a high severity score still deserves attention. Options C and D each name a single factor instead of the product.
  159. The survey shows exactly that pattern for the mobile deposit feature. Attractive in option A would produce delight when present, which is the opposite of what customers said. Indifferent in option C describes a feature customers do not care about, and reverse in option D describes a feature they would rather not have.
  160.   161. The correct answer is D (7 cards, based on demand during replenishment plus the buffer stock): During the 90 minute replenishment, the cell uses 60 times 1.5, which equals 90 parts. The two hour buffer adds 60 times 2, which equals 120 parts. The total is 90 plus 120, or 210 parts, and 210 divided by 30 parts per container equals 7 cards.   162.   163.   164. The correct answer is D (A third factor such as warm weather likely drives both measured values): A strong correlation shows that two values move together, not that one causes the other. Option B rejects a strong relationship for the wrong reason, since 0.92 is high. Option C assumes prediction is reliable without checking the model, and no such check is described.
  165. The correct answer is C (Project A, because it combines solid savings with a ready start date): Ranking weighs the benefit against feasibility and risk.   167. The correct answer is B (The process produces about 8 percent outside the limits, so it is not capable): Sixteen out of 200 readings is 0.08, or 8 percent outside the specification limits. Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Attack the 30 minutes of waiting, starting with the 12 minute credit check): The map shows 30 minutes of waiting against 12 minutes of work, so the largest chance to cut the 42 minute lead time sits in the waiting. The 12 minute credit check is the single largest waiting block, which makes it the natural first target. Option A works on value added time, which is the part the customer pays for and the smallest share. Option B sets an unrealistic target, and option D adds capacity to a step that is already a form of waste.
  2. The correct answer is D (Discuss the requests with the sponsor and follow the charter change process): Both requests sit outside the approved scope, and only the sponsor can authorize a change to the charter. Raising the requests there protects the project from scope creep while keeping the manager's concerns visible. Option A expands scope without approval. Option B hands the work to someone outside the team without a charter decision, and option C delays the decision until the control phase, when the team is closing out.
  3. The correct answer is A ($100,000, the total of scrap, rework, and warranty claim costs): The cost of poor quality collects internal failure costs and external failure costs. Scrap and rework are internal failures, and warranty claims are external failures, so the total is 60,000 plus 25,000 plus 15,000, which equals 100,000. Option B adds inspection, which is an appraisal cost and part of the cost of good quality. Option C reports only the appraisal figure, and option D leaves out the external failure cost.
  4. The correct answer is B (Lower medication errors from 14 to 7 per 10,000 doses by March 31): A usable charter goal names a metric, a baseline, a target, and a date. Option B gives all four, so progress can be measured. Option A gives activity rather than a result. Option C is broader but has no baseline or numeric target, and option D has a vague ending with no defined level. Only option B lets the team prove at the end whether the goal was met.
  5. The correct answer is C (The added variation will make the process less predictable and less capable): Reacting to common cause variation is tampering, and tampering adds variation to a stable process. Deming's funnel experiment showed that adjusting after every point grew the spread instead of shrinking it. Option A describes the result the operator expected, but the opposite happens. Option B assumes the adjustments steer the mean, which they cannot do when the variation is common cause. Option D is wrong because control limits come from the process, not from the number of subgroups.
  6. The correct answer is D (78 observations, based on the standard error formula for a mean): The sample size formula is z times s divided by the error, all squared. That is 1.96 times 9 divided by 2, which equals 8.82, and 8.82 squared is 77.79, which rounds up to 78. Option A squares the ratio of the deviation to the error but leaves out the z value, which gives about 20. Option B rounds 8.82 to 9 before squaring, which gives about 81, and option C uses a z value that does not match 95 percent confidence.
  7. The correct answer is A (Fill weight tends to fall as line speed rises, so speed merits further study): A downward trend with a fairly strong negative correlation shows the two variables move in opposite directions, and scatter plots reveal relationship, not cause. The result justifies a designed experiment that can test line speed while holding other factors steady. Option B ignores the visible pattern. Option C treats an observed relationship as proof of cause, and option D reverses the direction of the relationship shown in the chart.
  8. The correct answer is C (A procedural control, because it relies on a person following a check): A signed checklist depends on a person remembering and choosing to complete each step, which makes it a procedural control. It works only as well as the person's attention in the moment, and the manager's concern about rushed work points directly at that weakness. Option A describes a device that alerts, and no device is used. Option B describes a hard interlock, and option D describes a design change, and neither exists in this situation.
  9. The correct answer is B (Multivoting, so each member casts several votes on the short list): Multivoting gives every member a structured way to cast several votes across the five ideas, and the vote totals produce the ranked choice the Black Belt asked for. Option A commits the group to a majority before the quieter views are heard. Option C removes the team from the decision that it owns. Option D rewards the strongest speaker instead of producing a ranked list.
  10. The correct answer is D (DMADV, because it verifies a new design before it reaches full launch): DMADV stands for define, measure, analyze, design, and verify, and it is used when a new product or service is created. The goal here is to build quality into a new design before launch, which is exactly the DFSS case. DMAIC in option A improves a process that already exists. PDCA in option B is a general improvement cycle, and kaizen in option C delivers small improvements to current work.
  11. The correct answer is A (Update the control plan and standard work, then train the process owner): Control phase work transfers the improved process to the people who run it, and that transfer needs documented standards, a control plan, and training. The process owner is the person who will hold the gain after the team disbands. Option B keeps the team in place longer than the gain requires. Option C raises the target without changing how the work is controlled, and option D repeats the pilot instead of standardizing the new method.
  12. The correct answer is C (The supporting infrastructure, because projects need selection, sponsors, and review): Trained belts can do good work only when projects are selected, sponsored, and reviewed against business results. A missing pipeline, no finance review, and no sponsors describe a gap in that infrastructure, not a gap in training. Option A points at tools, which are not the missing element described. Option B asks for more belts when the existing ones have no projects, and option D blames the course even though the belts passed their exams.
  13. The correct answer is B (Gather data to test which listed causes actually drive the late releases): A fishbone diagram organizes possible causes, and the next step is to check the data to separate the real drivers from the rest. Option A jumps to a fix because it is easy, which is not evidence. Option C keeps adding ideas without testing any of them. Option D ranks branches by how many causes they hold, which says nothing about which cause actually affects release dates.
  14. The correct answer is D (The gauge resolution is too coarse, because it gives only four increments): Divide the process spread of 0.20 by the reading step of 0.05 and the gauge gives only four increments across the spread. The rule of tens looks for at least ten increments, so this gauge cannot see the process detail well enough. Option A and option B treat four increments as adequate, which ignores the rule. Option C reverses the problem, because a fine gauge would give more increments rather than fewer.
  15. The correct answer is C (Temperature has the larger main effect, at about 8.0 response units): The temperature effect is the average response at the high level, 70 plus 74 over 2, which is 72, minus the average at the low level, 62 plus 66 over 2, which is 64. The difference is 8.0. The catalyst effect is the average at the high level, 66 plus 74 over 2, which is 70, minus the average at the low level, 62 plus 70 over 2, which is 66, for 4.0. The interaction is (74 minus 70) minus (66 minus 62), which is zero. Temperature therefore has the larger main effect.
  16. The correct answer is B (Capability improved because the process variation around the target shrank): Cpk rises when the process spread shrinks or the mean moves toward the center of the specification. The question states that the mean stayed on target and the specification limits did not change, so the gain came from reduced variation. Option A contradicts the stated facts. Option C describes a study change rather than a process change, and option D describes tampering, which would add variation instead of reducing it.
  17. Reviewing productivity and quality together keeps speed and satisfaction in view at the same time. Option A drops the productivity measure entirely instead of balancing it. Option B adds a reminder without changing what is measured, and option C strengthens the single measure that caused the problem.
  18. The correct answer is A (Study the shift and restore stability, then recompute capability on stable data): Capability figures are meaningful only when the process is in statistical control. A control chart that shows a shift means the process changed during the study period, so the data mix two different processes and the Cpk value does not describe either one. Option B accepts a number that rests on unstable data. Option C widens limits to hide the shift, and option D collects more data from the same unsettled period.
  19. Option B changes alpha after the result, and option D uses a correlation method for a comparison of three group means.
  20. Option C ranks failure modes, and option D tests factors, so neither fits the first step of defining a process.
  21. The packing station limits the flow, so improving it lifts throughput for the whole line. Option A adds capacity ahead of a step that cannot absorb it. Option C adds more capacity at the wrong place, and option D slows the line below its current rate.
  22. The correct answer is C (The cycle time in seconds for each completed loan application): Continuous data can take any value on a scale, and a time in seconds does that. The other three measures are counts of whole items or events, so they are discrete. Option A counts returns, option B counts defects, and option D counts shifts until a breakdown. Each of those can only take whole number values, which is why they are not continuous.
  23.   24. The correct answer is C (Define check-in time as 3 minutes or less from arrival to room key): A measurable requirement states what will be observed and the level that counts as success.   25. Option C states a false limit on the tool, and option D describes the opposite problem, since the team stopped too early rather than asking too many questions.

  27.   28.   29. People who only need updates can receive them without holding a seat at every decision. Option A adds another facilitator to a team that is already too large. Option B splits a narrow scope into two groups and adds coordination work, and option D moves the deadline instead of fixing the team design.
  30. The correct answer is D (About 86 percent, based on run time against the planned production time): Availability is run time divided by planned production time.   31. The correct answer is C (1 minute per call, because available time equals the number of calls): Takt time is available work time divided by customer demand. Eight hours of planned work is 480 minutes, and 480 divided by 480 calls is 1 minute per call. Option A divides the demand by the number of agents, which is not takt. Option B uses the shift length by itself, and option D splits demand across shifts that the question does not describe.
  32. The correct answer is B (Translate customer needs into ranked design requirements and targets): A house of quality maps customer requirements against design features so the team can see which features matter most and where to set targets. That ranking guides concept work before any design is fixed. Option A describes verification after launch. Option C describes an FMEA, and option D describes process capability work on manufacturing, which comes later than setting design priorities.
  33. The correct answer is C (Hoshin planning, which cascades top-level goals into linked actions and measures.): Hoshin planning sets a small number of breakthrough goals and works backward into targets, actions, and measures at each level of the organization. That matches the movement from the annual goal to quarterly targets and departmental actions. Kaizen works at a much smaller scale, benchmarking compares the bank with an outside performer without setting internal targets, and DMAIC fixes an existing process rather than deploying a strategy across the business.
  34. The correct answer is A (The customer perspective, which tracks satisfaction, retention, and service quality.): The balanced scorecard asks leaders to watch several views of the business, and the customer view covers how well the organization keeps and satisfies the people who buy from it. Quietly losing long-time clients is the signal that view is designed to catch. The financial view is the one the firm already watches, the internal process view would show slow or defective work rather than client departures, and the learning and growth view covers employee skills and culture.
  35. The correct answer is D (Weak leadership engagement, which removes sponsorship and visible priority.): A Six Sigma program stalls when leaders do not sponsor the work, and a plant manager who skips reviews and withholds support signals that projects are optional. Sponsorship supplies resources, clears obstacles, and ties the effort to business goals. Weak measurement systems and thin statistical training would slow projects after they start, and unclear requirements would show up inside charters, so none of those fit a manager who gives no visible backing.
  36. The correct answer is B (Prioritizing the project portfolio against agreed selection criteria.): Portfolio selection scores candidate projects on benefits and feasibility, then ranks them so limited Black Belt time goes to the best options. Combining impact, fit, risk, and speed is that kind of decision. Putting six problems into one team would spread effort thin, a hypothesis test compares data rather than ranking proposals, and a control plan is written after a process change rather than before a project is chosen.
  37. The correct answer is A (Map every step of the process and set measures for the whole flow.): A process owner is accountable for the entire process rather than one department, so the first job is to see the whole flow and place measures on it that cross department lines. That shows where the three weeks are actually spent. Assigning blame, buying software for one queue, or moving staff all treat a local step as if it were the whole process and leave the handoffs untouched.
  38. The correct answer is C (35,000 dollars, the internal failure and external failure costs together.): Cost of poor quality covers money spent because work went wrong, which is internal failure plus external failure. Scrap and rework are internal failure at 25,000 dollars, and customer returns and warranty work are external failure at 10,000 dollars, so 25,000 plus 10,000 equals 35,000 dollars. Inspection and testing are appraisal costs and supplier training is a prevention cost, so both sit outside the poor quality total even though they are quality costs.
  39. The correct answer is D (A process benchmark, which studied a different industry known for its method.): Benchmarking against a direct competitor is competitive benchmarking, while studying a very different organization that performs a similar process well is process benchmarking. The pharmacy dispenses orders with high accuracy, so the center is copying a method rather than matching a rival. An internal benchmark compares sites inside the same company, and a strategic benchmark looks at how another organization changed its overall direction, so neither fits the pharmacy study.
  40. The correct answer is B (Track weekly call abandonment and first contact resolution as leading indicators.): Leading indicators move before the customer outcome does, so weekly abandonment and first contact resolution warn the director while there is still time to act. A monthly satisfaction score is a lagging measure because it reports the result after customers have decided. Surveying less often, waiting for an annual study, or lengthening the monthly survey all keep the director looking backward at results rather than forward at the process.
  41. The correct answer is C (Storming, where members test roles and work through open disagreement.): Teams normally move from forming into storming, and storming shows up as conflict about roles, methods, and whose information can be trusted. The arguing and distrust in this team are the classic signs of that stage. Forming is the polite early stage before real work begins, norming comes after the group settles its rules, and performing is the steady stage that follows.
  42. The correct answer is A (A team charter, which defines the team's purpose, scope, and operating rules.): A team charter records why the team exists, what it may change, who is on it, when it reports, and how it will decide things. Writing that page before data collection is standard practice because it prevents later arguments about scope and authority. A control plan is written near the end of a project, a data collection plan covers measurement details only, and a work breakdown structure lists tasks without stating purpose or boundaries.
  43. The correct answer is D (Guiding the team through constructive conflict toward a data based decision.): Constructive conflict uses disagreement to test ideas, and asking each side to restate the other position forces real listening before anyone argues back. Looking for data then turns the argument into a question the team can answer. Suppressing the conflict, escalating it to the sponsor, or calling an immediate vote all close the discussion down before the team has examined which measure serves the project.
  44. The correct answer is B (The nominal group technique, which ranks ideas without group pressure.): The nominal group technique asks members to generate and rank ideas in writing before any discussion, which keeps a talkative or senior member from steering the list. The team then tallies the private rankings and discusses the leaders. A cause and effect diagram sorts causes rather than ranking ideas, force field analysis weighs forces for and against a change, and a process decision program chart maps countermeasures for a chosen plan.
  45. The correct answer is C (Team health measures, such as trust, participation, and member satisfaction.): Project performance has two sides, the results the process delivers and the health of the team that delivered them. Members who would avoid the leader again are telling the sponsor that trust and participation were poor even though the numbers looked fine. Capability, financial return, and schedule measures all describe the work product, which the team already passed, so none of them reveal the problem described.
  46. The correct answer is A (It combines stated needs with observed behavior, giving a fuller customer view.): Voice of the customer work is stronger when it pairs what customers say with what they actually do, so complaint letters and focus groups supply stated needs while the return rate supplies behavior. Together they show where the service fails and how much it matters. A single survey would capture only stated opinion, and no mix of sources removes the duty to translate needs into measurable requirements.
  47. The correct answer is D (A delighter, which creates satisfaction when present and no complaint when absent.): The Kano model sorts features by how they affect satisfaction. Must be requirements are taken for granted, performance attributes satisfy in step with how much is delivered, and delighters please customers when they appear without causing complaint when they are missing. Free parking that generated delight and was not requested fits the delighter category, while the clean room and the faster discharge fit the other categories named in the survey.
  48. The correct answer is C (A critical to quality requirement, which translates the wish into a measure.): A critical to quality requirement takes a vague customer wish and states it in numbers the process can be measured against, so a request for hot food becomes a temperature with a target and a lower limit. Each requirement also needs an owner so someone acts when the measure drifts. A complaint category groups feedback, a process map shows flow, and a control chart is used after a measure has been defined.
  49. A control plan and a data collection plan are working documents written inside the project, and a quality function deployment matrix links customer needs to design features.

Accepting the work without that check spreads the team thin and is how scope creep starts. Simply declining hides a real business need, and splitting the team three ways would slow the original goal without authority to do so.

  53. The correct answer is A (Weight is continuous data, and invoice accuracy is discrete attribute data.): Continuous data can take any value on a scale, so a weight in kilograms can show small differences between shipments. Attribute data sorts items into categories, so the invoice either matched the order or it did not. Swapping the two types fails for the same reason, and calling both continuous or both discrete ignores the difference between a measurement scale and a category.
  54. The correct answer is C (Fifty percent, which divides 0.4 by 0.8 and multiplies the result by 100.): Percent gauge repeatability and reproducibility compares the variation the measurement system adds with the total variation the study sees, so 0.4 divided by 0.8 equals 0.5, and 0.5 times 100 gives 50 percent. Common guidance treats a value under 10 percent as acceptable and a value above 30 percent as needing work, so 50 percent points to a measurement system that needs attention. Dividing total variation by gauge variation inverts the ratio, and the other options divide by the wrong quantity.
  55. The correct answer is B (Stability, which shows how a measurement system behaves over time.): Stability tracks the same master or standard over an extended period, so a slow drift in readings on the same check weight points at the measurement system rather than the process. Linearity describes accuracy across the range of the instrument, while repeatability and reproducibility describe variation within one operator and between operators during a single study.

That matters here because the shifts run with different operators. A simple random sample ignores the shift structure, a systematic sample follows a fixed interval along the line, and a convenience sample uses whichever bags are easiest to reach.

Here 1.96 times 2 equals 3.92, and 3.92 divided by 0.5 equals 7.84. Squaring 7.84 gives 61.47, which rounds up to 62 observations.   60. The correct answer is A (1.00, which comes from dividing 6 by three times the standard deviation.): Cpk looks at the distance from the process mean to each specification limit in units of three standard deviations and takes the smaller of the two values. Here 56 minus 50 is 6, and 50 minus 44 is also 6, so each side gives 6 divided by 6, which equals 1.00. Because the process is centered, Cp and Cpk agree at 1.00. That benchmark comes from the usual 1.5 sigma shift, which allows for the drift a stable process shows over the long term.   62. The pattern gives the team a strong candidate for further study.   63. The correct answer is A (Reject the null hypothesis, since the p value is below the alpha level.): The p value is the chance of seeing a difference this large when the null hypothesis is true, and when it falls below alpha the team rejects the null and calls the difference statistically significant. Here 0.03 is below 0.05, so the two sites differ by more than chance alone would explain. The p value is not a percentage difference between the sites, and the test is not set aside because the result sits close to the line.
  64. The correct answer is B (2.0, which comes from dividing the 4 minute difference by 2 minutes.): A two sample t statistic compares the difference between the sample means with the standard error of that difference, so 34 minus 30 equals 4, and 4 divided by 2 equals 2.0. That value is then compared with a critical value from the t table. Reporting the raw difference, dividing a mean by the standard error, or inverting the ratio all use the right numbers in the wrong relationship.
  65. The correct answer is D (A one way analysis of variance, since it compares more than two group means.): Analysis of variance tests whether the means of three or more groups could all come from a single population, which suits four suppliers measured on the same response. A t test handles only two groups, a chi square test works with counts in categories rather than averages, and linear regression fits a line through continuous predictors rather than comparing group means.
  66. A chi square test of independence compares the counts observed in each cell with the counts expected if the variables were unrelated. The t test and analysis of variance compare averages that this data does not contain, and a gauge study examines a measurement system rather than a relationship between survey variables.

  118. The correct answer is D (A special cause is present, so the team should find its source.): A point beyond a control limit is the standard signal of a special cause, and at 13.6 it sits well above the upper limit of 13.0, so the team should search for an assignable source such as a material lot, an operator, or a setup change. One proven special cause does not leave the process stable, and a single point does not make the variation common cause. Control limits come from the process and are not loosened to hide a point.
  119. The correct answer is A (Median evening prescription wait is 74 minutes, above the 40 minute target.): A problem statement describes the gap between what is happening and what is required, using numbers and a scope, so a measured wait of 74 minutes against a 40 minute target fits. The other options state a feeling, a comparison with an unrelated topic, or a proposed solution, and a problem statement should not contain the answer.
  120. The correct answer is B (Agree on one operational definition of a late order for both shifts.): When two groups use different rules to decide what counts as late, the gap in the numbers may come from the definition rather than from the process, so the definition must be fixed before any comparison. Testing a difference that comes from the measurement rule wastes the effort. The other options act on a number that is not yet trustworthy.
  121. The correct answer is B (A five whys chain that moves from the symptom to a root cause.): The five whys method asks why in sequence, peeling back layers from the visible symptom until the team reaches a cause it can act on, which is how scratches led to the missing maintenance task. Pareto analysis ranks problems but does not dig into one chain, FMEA scores risks before failures happen, and a control chart monitors variation over time.
  122. The correct answer is A (Publish each decision with its rationale and give absent members a set response window.): When some members cannot join live, recording the decision, the reasoning, and an agreed window for objections keeps everyone in the loop and stops the same item from being reopened. Forcing attendance across time zones strains the team, and deciding without the absent members or dropping meetings loses the input that makes a decision stick.
  123. The correct answer is C (Two hours of press time per shift become available for production.): Each changeover falls by 60 minutes, and with two changeovers per shift that frees 120 minutes, which is two hours for making parts. A shorter setup does not change the run rate or raise quality by itself, and batch size is a separate decision the team may revisit later.
  124. The correct answer is C (An audit schedule that checks the process owner's measures over time.): A control plan tells people what to monitor, but without periodic audits and a review of the measures the process drifts back toward the old way. A presentation and a rejected-idea list are records of past work, and a new charter starts a different project rather than sustaining this one.
  125. The correct answer is D (Payback is about 4 months, so the project clears the requirement.): Payback equals the project cost divided by the yearly savings, so $80,000 divided by $240,000 is 0.33 of a year, or about 4 months, which is well inside the 12 month rule. The 8, 12, and 24 month answers do not come from this division. A payback this short means the project returns its cost within the first year.
  126. The correct answer is A (Measure a larger sample of cycle times from the same process.): The width of a confidence interval shrinks as the sample size grows, because the standard error is the standard deviation divided by the square root of n. Raising the confidence level widens the interval, rounding adds measurement error without adding information, and sampling one shift narrows the scope of what the estimate covers rather than tightening it.
  127. The correct answer is B (160, the product of severity, occurrence, and detection for this mode.): The risk priority number is the product of the three ratings, so 8 times 5 times 4 equals 160. Adding the ratings gives 17, using only severity and occurrence gives 40, and squaring the top rating before adding the rest gives 73. Each of those routes leaves out a rating or applies the wrong operation.
  128. The correct answer is D (Run a kaizen event that tests the fix in a short, focused session.): When the scope is small, the cause is understood, and one team can act quickly, a kaizen event delivers the change in days instead of months, which is what the DMAIC roadmap is scaled for. Claiming that every improvement needs the full roadmap overstates the method, waiting for funding delays a simple fix, and the process clearly exists, so a design project does not apply.
  129. The correct answer is D (Find and remove the special causes, then confirm the process is stable.): A capability index describes a stable process, so special causes must be found and removed first and the chart must then show only common-cause variation. Removing inconvenient points or widening the limits hides the problem instead of solving it. Cp assumes stability just as much as Cpk does.
  130. The correct answer is B (A kappa statistic, which adjusts agreement for what chance would produce.): With pass or fail data, some agreement happens by luck, so kappa compares the observed agreement with the agreement expected from chance alone and gives a fairer view of the gauge. A standard deviation suits measured values, a control limit belongs to a chart over time, and a capability index describes the process against specifications rather than the inspectors against each other.
  131. The correct answer is C (A third factor such as customer traffic may drive both measures at once.): A correlation shows that two measures move together, but a third factor such as busy lunch hours can raise both the wait time and the number of tellers scheduled, so acting on the pair alone can mislead. Correlation values between zero and one are normal and useful, so option A misstates the statistic. The data do not show that waits cause staffing, and negative correlations need no reversal to be read.
  132. The correct answer is C (Run a pilot at a second site with different conditions and review the results.): A second pilot at a site with different conditions tests whether the fix depends on the first site's people, equipment, or volume, which is the main risk in a wide rollout. Launching everywhere at once multiplies the cost of a failure, waiting a year delays a proven gain, and training everyone before the process is confirmed can spread a faulty method.
  133. The correct answer is A (Ask finance to validate the savings figures before they are published to leaders): The body of knowledge gives finance a role in supporting a project and confirming its outcome, so claimed savings get an independent check before leaders see them. Publishing the figure first and correcting it later weakens trust in the whole program. A percentage figure alone hides the money that the business needs to justify the project. Waiting a year loses the chance to use proven gains to fund the next project.
  134. The correct answer is C (A contingency plan that names the trigger, the backup vendor, and the recovery steps): Strategic planning in the body of knowledge includes contingency and business continuity planning, which prepare the plant for a vendor that cannot deliver. A progress report tracks work but does not prepare a response when the vendor fails. A Pareto chart studies defect causes rather than supplier risk. A communication plan tells people about the change but does not protect the schedule.
  135. The correct answer is B (A strength, weakness, opportunity, and threat analysis of the current position): A SWOT analysis sorts internal factors, the staffing shortage and the good reputation, from external ones, the new competitor and rising supply costs. That split is what makes the analysis useful for strategy. A value stream map studies how work flows through a process rather than the market position. A control plan and a return on investment model both come later, after the strategy is set.
  136. The correct answer is D (Run a stakeholder analysis and readiness assessment, then plan the communication): Change management in the body of knowledge starts with stakeholder analysis and a readiness assessment, which show who can block the change and what they need to hear. Escalating first uses authority before the cause of the resistance is understood. Moving the pilot hides the problem instead of solving it, and more training does not answer managers who call the change optional.
  137. The correct answer is A (Competitive benchmarking against the rival, and collaborative benchmarking with the trade group): Competitive benchmarking compares performance with a direct rival, and collaborative benchmarking shares data with partners that are not competitors. Calling the rival study internal is wrong because the rival sits outside the company. Best practice and breakthrough benchmarking describe the kind of gain a team seeks, not the partner it studies, so neither name fits both activities.
  138. The correct answer is B (The customers, the meter field staff, the billing group, and the regulator): The body of knowledge asks for the impact of a project on customers, suppliers, and other stakeholders, so the report covers everyone the change touches. The vendor matters, but the project team and the vendor together leave out the people who live with the meters and the bills. Customers are central, yet the field staff, the billing group, and the regulator also feel the change.
  139. The correct answer is C (The overtime figure is a hard saving, and the goodwill figure is a soft benefit): Hard savings come from profit and loss accounts, and the overtime money has already left the budget, so finance can count it. Soft benefits such as estimated goodwill are real but harder to verify. Calling both figures hard overstates the case, and calling both soft understates it. Treating the goodwill estimate as hard because of a budget entry mixes the two categories.
  140. The correct answer is A (Treat the coaching count as a leading measure and revenue as a lagging measure): Leading indicators move before the result and tell leaders whether the plan is working, while lagging measures confirm what already happened. Coaching conversations are a behavior that should drive revenue later. Treating both as lagging misses the early signal, and keeping revenue alone removes the line of sight from daily work to the goal.
  141. The correct answer is D (Direct the team with clear tasks and close guidance while skills are low): A newly formed team with low familiarity needs direction, which is the leadership style that supplies clear tasks and close guidance. Delegating asks a team to run itself before it has the skills or the shared habits. Supporting and coaching fit later stages, once members know each other and the work.
  142. The correct answer is B (Assess the current skills gap, then write learning objectives for the course): Training design in the body of knowledge starts with a needs assessment that finds the skills gap and sets learning objectives. Booking the room or buying a standard package decides the delivery before anyone knows what the staff need. Measuring after the class is evaluation, which comes at the end rather than first.
  143. The correct answer is C (Meet the engineer privately, then set a ground rule that every idea gets airtime): A dominant participant can shut down a group, and the body of knowledge lists intervention as a conflict tool. A private conversation avoids a public fight, and a ground rule on airtime protects the quieter members. Letting the engineer speak first rewards the interruption. A silent ballot removes the discussion that a design review needs.
  144. The correct answer is B (A virtual team that shares data and holds short meetings across the three shifts): Team type follows the constraints, and operators on three shifts cannot meet as one group, so a virtual team that works through shared data and short calls fits. A self-directed team still needs contact to set its direction. A group that meets at handover competes with the changeover work, and a day shift team leaves the other shifts out.
  145. The correct answer is A (Hands-on practice with the gauge, followed by guided feedback on real parts): Adult learners bring experience, so practice with feedback on real parts connects the training to the job. A longer slide deck adds theory the machinists do not need. A manual read before the first run leaves them alone with a new tool, and a recorded lecture removes the coaching that a measurement routine needs.
  146. The correct answer is D (The plant supervisors and the payroll clerks who both feed the process): Customers include the internal groups whose work depends on the process, so the supervisors who submit timesheets and the clerks who receive them both count. The employees paid are also customers, but naming only them misses the internal chain. The software vendor supplies the tool rather than using the process.
  147. The correct answer is C (A process decision program chart that pairs each step with a countermeasure): A process decision program chart, often shortened to PDPC, lists what can go wrong at each step of a plan and the response for each problem. A work breakdown structure divides the work, and a Gantt chart shows timing. A prioritization matrix ranks options, which is not the same as planning a response for each step.
  148. The correct answer is A (Translate the quoted needs into measurable customer requirements for the project): Customer data collection in the Define phase ends by turning what customers said into requirements the team can measure. Repeating the interviews adds no new information. Jumping to the measure phase skips the translation step, and sending the quotes to marketing uses the data for promotion rather than for the project.
  149. The correct answer is B (A work breakdown structure that divides the deliverables into work packages): A work breakdown structure splits the deliverables into work packages small enough for one person to own, which is the step before estimating durations. A Gantt chart shows the schedule that comes later. A RACI table assigns roles for decisions, and a tollgate schedule sets review points, so neither one splits the work.
  150. The correct answer is D (The survey relied on memory and estimates rather than on measured times): Observation with a stopwatch records what actually happened, while a survey asks customers to recall a wait that they may overstate or understate. The number of customers reached is a sample size question, not the weakness being fixed here. Leading questions and the choice of day are real survey risks, but the stopwatch replaces the estimate rather than the questions.
  151. The correct answer is C (Take the request to the sponsor so the scope and charter can be reviewed): The body of knowledge calls for periodic charter reviews with stakeholders, which is how a change in scope gets an owner's decision. Adding the work quietly abandons the charter the sponsor approved, and assigning it to spare time does the same. Refusing outright blocks a request that the sponsor may well accept.
  152. The correct answer is A (The sample covers one season, so it may not represent usage across the year): Data collection plans have to account for seasonality, and January gas use in a cold month is a poor stand-in for a yearly average. Sample size is not the problem, since six weeks of daily readings give plenty of points. Gas usage is one measurement type, and daily readings can be summed into monthly totals.
  153. The correct answer is B (The two peaks suggest two sources of output, so the team should stratify): A histogram with two peaks points to a mixed population, such as two fill heads running at different settings, so the team should split the data by source before judging capability. The process is not centered on one peak, and the shape is not normal, so a single capability index would mislead. The dip between the peaks is a clue about the mixture rather than proof of extra random variation.
  154. The correct answer is A (The coefficient of variation, found by dividing each standard deviation by its mean): The coefficient of variation expresses spread as a share of the mean, so it lets a team compare measures that use different units. Fill weight gives 2 divided by 200, or 1 percent, and cap torque gives 0.6 divided by 6, or 10 percent. The range, the median, and the count do not put the two spreads on a common scale.
  155. The correct answer is D (The two regions define the same measure differently, so the scores are not comparable): Measurement systems have to be consistent across the organization before their numbers are compared. One region counts a parcel as on time by the promised date and the other by noon of that same date, so the western figure is harder to earn. Neither definition is wrong on its own, and letting each region choose its own standard keeps the comparison broken.
  156. The correct answer is C (The difference between describing the change and inferring what caused it): The before and after totals describe what happened, while the claim that training caused the drop is an inference that needs evidence, such as a comparison group or a check for other changes in the same period. Sampling and census terms do not fit a single line. Discrete and continuous measures are a data type question rather than a cause question.
  157. The correct answer is B (0.01, found by multiplying 0.10 by 0.10 for independent events): The multiplication rule for independent events says the chance that both occur is the product of the separate chances, so 0.10 times 0.10 gives 0.01. Adding the two chances gives 0.20, which answers a different question. Averaging has no basis in probability, and 0.19 is the chance that at least one sensor fails.
  158. The correct answer is D (The Weibull distribution, which models time to failure with changing hazard rates): The body of knowledge lists the Weibull among the distributions for reliability data, and its shape parameter describes a failure rate that rises or falls over time. The binomial counts successes in a fixed number of trials and the Poisson counts rare events in an interval, so neither models time to failure. The chi square distribution compares observed and expected counts.
  159. The correct answer is A (100 orders, since the standard error falls with the square root of the sample size): The standard error equals the standard deviation divided by the square root of the sample size, so 10 divided by the square root of 25 gives 2 minutes. Reaching 1 minute means the square root of the sample size must be 10, which gives 100 orders. Doubling the sample does not halve the standard error, since the square root of 50 is about 7.07.
  160. The correct answer is C (The manager read causation into a correlation that could run either direction): A correlation of plus 0.9 says the two counts move together, and the cause can run the other way, with plants that find more defects hiring more inspectors. The formula is not the problem, since the correlation comes from the data. Both counts are numerical, and plant size is one possible explanation, but the core error is the jump from a correlation to a cause.
  161. The correct answer is B (The next single trip can vary more than the average time for that route): A confidence interval covers the mean response for a route, while a prediction interval covers one future trip, so it must be wider to capture individual variation around the line. A wide prediction interval is normal rather than a sign that the model fits poorly. Widening the confidence interval would only hide the difference between the mean and a single trip.
  162. The correct answer is D (The three largest causes together hold about 85 percent of the exceptions): Adding the top three counts gives 46 plus 24 plus 15, which is 85 of the 100 exceptions, so the vital few cover most of the problem. The counts are ranked causes from one week of data rather than random variation. Wrong item alone holds 46 percent, and the two smallest causes hold only 15 percent, so they do not need a separate project.
  163. The correct answer is A (The chance of declaring an effect when the process has not actually changed): Alpha is the chance of a type I error, which means rejecting the null hypothesis when it is true, so the 0.05 setting limits how often the team claims an improvement that is not real. Missing a real effect is a type II error, which beta controls. The distance between the sample mean and the process mean is sampling error, and the p-value will move with new data for reasons this setting does not control.
  164. The correct answer is B (A multivariate analysis of variance that tests the four measures together): A multivariate analysis of variance, or MANOVA, compares groups on several correlated responses at once and holds the overall error rate in line. Four separate one-way tests would raise the chance of at least one false alarm. A chi square test works with counts, and a regression on a grouping code treats the categories as numbers.
  165. The correct answer is C (A design FMEA for the valve and a process FMEA for the assembly line): A design FMEA studies how a product can fail before the design is frozen, and a process FMEA studies how the manufacturing process can fail. The line that installs the valve backward is a process concern, so swapping the two names in option D is wrong. A fault tree, a Pareto chart, a design review, and a capability study are different tools with different purposes.
  166. The correct answer is D (0.126, found by adding the two probabilities and subtracting their product): The top event happens if at least one sub-system fails, so the chance is 0.08 plus 0.05 minus the overlap of 0.08 times 0.05, which is 0.004, giving 0.126. Adding the two probabilities alone double counts the case where both fail. Multiplying gives the chance that both fail rather than the chance of either, and averaging the two values has no basis in probability.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Over-processing, since the extra reviews add work that the customer does not value): The seven wastes include over-processing, which is work that adds steps without adding value, and two signatures beyond the one that carries authority fit that description. Inventory, motion, and waiting describe other waste patterns, such as files piling up or traveling between desks. The decision itself does not change with the extra reviews, which is the sign of over-processing.
  2. The correct answer is C (Randomization, which runs the formulas in a random order to spread outside effects): Randomizing the run order spreads a slow drift in the mixer across all four formulas instead of loading it onto whichever formula goes last. Repetition repeats a treatment, and blocking groups runs that share a known nuisance factor. Resolution describes how much confounding a fractional design carries, which is a property of the plan rather than protection against drift.
  3. The correct answer is B (Heijunka, which levels the mix of the three types across each day): Heijunka levels the production mix so the plant makes small amounts of each type often, which cuts the pile of finished goods and shortens the wait for orders. The changeover already takes only five minutes, so shortening it is not the missing piece. Poka-yoke prevents errors and total productive maintenance protects equipment, and neither one levels the schedule.
  4. The correct answer is D (The two factors interact, so the effect of temperature depends on pressure): The temperature effect changes from plus 12 units at low pressure to minus 4 units at high pressure, which is an interaction between the two factors. An interaction means the effect of one factor depends on the level of the other. Temperature does not act the same way at both pressures, and pressure does not carry the larger main effect, since its average effect is minus 4 against plus 4 for temperature. A third factor is not needed to explain the reversal.
  5. The correct answer is A (Move to continuous flow so units pass through the cell one at a time): Value added work takes 20 minutes while waiting between steps adds 300 minutes, so the delay sits in the queues that batch processing creates. Moving to continuous flow, where units pass through the cell one at a time, removes most of that waiting. A second shift or a faster last machine adds capacity without touching queue time, and a larger batch makes the waiting longer.
  6. The correct answer is B (Speeding up the upstream stations will build extra inventory without raising output): The plant can only ship what the constraint produces, so improving stations that already run faster than the constraint adds inventory and cost without adding throughput. The constraint sets the pace for the whole plant. Option A assumes the upstream stations limit output, which they do not, and option C reverses cause and direction, since the constraint rate does not follow the upstream stations. Extra inventory also raises rather than lowers operating expense.
  7. The correct answer is D (The enlarged hole, since prevention stops the error before it can happen): Error proofing is strongest when the wrong action becomes physically impossible, which is what the redesigned hole does. A sensor that flags a wrong bolt after the bracket is fixed detects the error after the work is done, so the part still needs rework. Detection and prevention are not equally strong, and the sensor option carries no cost advantage that changes the ranking.
  8. The correct answer is C (Resolution, which describes the aliasing built into a fractional design): A fractional factorial runs only part of the full set of combinations, so some effects are aliased, or entangled, with each other, and the resolution of the design states how severe that entanglement is. Six factors in a full two-level factorial would need sixty-four runs, so a sixteen-run plan is a quarter fraction. A design of resolution IV keeps main effects clear while letting some two-factor interactions share an estimate. Randomization, blocking, and replication are protections in a design, but none describes which effects are entangled.
  9. The correct answer is B (A chart that codes each part's readings as deviations from its own target): Short-run process control expresses each reading as a deviation from that part's target, which lets one chart cover many part numbers with few pieces each. A p chart, a c chart, and an np chart all work with counts of defects or defectives in a sample, so none fits small lots of many different parts.
  10. The correct answer is A (A steady upward trend of that length signals a special cause on the chart): Seven or more points moving steadily in one direction is a runs rule signal, so the process shows a special cause even though every point sits inside the limits. Waiting for a point to cross a limit would let the drift continue. The limits come from the process data rather than being too wide, and a steady trend is not the random pattern that common cause variation produces.
  11. The correct answer is C (A visual control that shows the correct setting at the point of use): Visual controls such as color-coded parts that fit only the correct position tell an operator the right setting at a glance, before any part is cut. A control chart checks results after the fact, and a standard work sheet describes the steps without stopping a wrong setup. Total productive maintenance keeps equipment reliable, which is a different goal.
  12. The correct answer is D (An exponentially weighted moving average chart that reacts to small shifts): The exponentially weighted moving average chart, often called EWMA, weights recent readings more heavily and detects small shifts sooner than an individuals chart. A p chart counts defectives in a sample, which this process does not produce. An individuals and moving range chart is the chart that already missed the shift, and a short-run chart codes readings against a target rather than improving sensitivity.
  13. The correct answer is A (Pick the characteristics that matter most to the customer and link to the process output): The body of knowledge asks the team to select the critical process characteristics for control chart monitoring, and the ones that matter most to the customer and drive the output are the critical few. Familiarity, frequency in a report, and questions from new staff are practical concerns that can point to useful measures, but they do not identify which characteristic controls the customer's result.
  14. The correct answer is C (Update the posted work instructions so they match the revised method): Sustained improvement depends on documents such as work instructions and standard operating procedures that match the new method, so the posted card has to change. Retraining alone leaves the old card in place as a second, conflicting instruction. Approving the old card keeps the wrong steps in service, and hiding it leaves the method undocumented.
  15. The correct answer is B (DMADOV, which adds an optimize step between the design and validate phases): DMADOV extends the design roadmap with an optimization step, which is what the stakeholders are asking for before launch. DMADV stops at validate, so it does not include that step. DMAIC improves an existing process rather than designing a new service, and PDCA is a general improvement cycle rather than a DFSS roadmap.
  16. The correct answer is D (Design for maintainability, which makes service work faster after the sale): Design for X covers constraints such as maintainability, which shapes how quickly and cheaply a product can be serviced. A side panel that lets a technician replace a seal in ten minutes instead of a full teardown is a maintainability choice. Design for cost, design for test, and design for producibility address assembly cost, inspection, and building the product rather than service access.
  17. The correct answer is D (A hoshin kanri plan that cascades annual goals into project charters.): Hoshin kanri is the policy deployment method that takes the annual strategy and breaks it into targets and projects that each level of the organization owns. That cascade is what ties project selection to the strategy instead of leaving the choice to local preference. A training calendar builds skill but does not link projects to goals. A software license and a newsletter are support activities that do not govern which projects get selected.
  18. The correct answer is D (Benchmarking, which compares the process against a reference and adapts the findings.): Benchmarking studies a reference process, often at a leading organization, and uses the comparison to set targets and transfer practices that fit the local setting. Brainstorming and kaizen generate ideas from inside the organization, and an audit checks compliance with existing procedures. None of those three reaches outside for a performance standard, so they do not describe the comparison the Black Belt proposed.
  19. The correct answer is B (The process owner, who sustains the gains and answers for the process results.): After the project closes, the person who runs the process takes responsibility for the control plan, the charts, and the response when a signal fires. The champion sponsors the project and removes barriers while it runs. The master black belt provides coaching and statistical support, and the green belt executes smaller projects. Neither role owns the day to day process results after handover.
  20. The correct answer is B (No management system selects, sponsors, and reviews projects against business goals.): Training alone does not produce results. Deployment needs a system that picks projects with a real business case, assigns a champion, and reviews progress against the goals. Exam dates, workbook supplies, and the order in which statistics topics are taught are administrative details that do not stall a deployment on their own.
  21. The correct answer is C (The balanced scorecard, which groups measures into financial, customer, process, and learning views.): The balanced scorecard links measures across four perspectives so a team can see whether improvement in one area helps or hurts another. A control chart, a Pareto chart, and a run chart each display data on one measure, and none of them links categories of performance to the strategy.
  22. The correct answer is B (A SIPOC diagram, which frames the process boundary from supplier to customer.): SIPOC stands for suppliers, inputs, process, outputs, and customers, and it gives a one page boundary view before detailed mapping begins. A Pareto chart ranks causes, a control chart tracks variation, and a Gantt chart schedules tasks. None of those three defines where the process starts and stops.
  23. The correct answer is A (Divide 40,000 dollars by 12,000 dollars, which gives 3.3 years.): Simple payback is the initial cost divided by the annual savings. Here 40,000 divided by 12,000 equals 3.33, so the project recovers its cost in just over three years. Multiplication, addition, and subtraction do not produce a payback period in years, and the subtraction result of 28,000 dollars is a gap, not a duration.
  24. The correct answer is C (Calls completed per hour together with the rate of repeat contacts for the same issue.): Efficiency is captured by calls completed per hour, and the customer outcome is captured by how often customers must come back about the same problem. Agent counts, call length, and supervisor counts describe resources rather than results, so those pairs would not show whether the transfers are harming customers.
  25. The correct answer is D (Storming, where members test boundaries and disagree about roles and methods.): Storming is the stage where members push against each other and question who does what. Disagreement about data and approach is normal here, and the leader guides the group toward shared rules. Forming is polite and cautious, norming brings agreement on rules, and performing is the stage of smooth joint problem solving.
  26. The correct answer is D (A team charter or ground rules list that the members write and follow.): A charter states the team purpose, the decision rules, and the behaviors members agree to follow, and writing it together builds ownership. A control plan and a work breakdown structure serve other purposes, and a cause and effect diagram sorts causes rather than setting team agreements.
  27. The correct answer is B (Active listening and reflection, which lowers the heat before the team resolves the issue.): Asking each person to restate the other's view forces real listening and separates the technical disagreement from the personal tone. Smoothing hides the issue, forcing uses rank, and withdrawal defers the topic. All three leave the underlying dispute unresolved, which is why the reflective approach fits better here.
  28. The correct answer is D (Use round robin prompts so each member speaks before the team decides.): A round robin gives every member a turn, which draws out the quiet members who hold process knowledge and stops a few voices from dominating. Email after the meeting weakens the discussion, cutting the team loses expertise, and letting the talkative members lead would keep the same imbalance in place.
  29. The correct answer is C (Public credit at a company meeting and a written note to each member's manager.): Recognition does not have to be cash. Public credit and a note to each member's manager reward the team at low cost and reinforce the behavior the company wants repeated. A bonus that does not exist, vacation for one person, and new software miss the goal of thanking the whole team for the result.
  30. The correct answer is C (The voice of the customer, which turns patient needs into measurable requirements.): Voice of the customer work collects what customers want through interviews, surveys, and complaint data, then translates those needs into measurable requirements called CTQs. Measurement system analysis, capability studies, and experiments come later, when the team measures and tests the process rather than gathering what customers value.
  31. The correct answer is B (On time delivery fell from 94 to 87 percent between January and June this year.): A useful problem statement names the gap, its size, and the time frame, and it avoids a guessed cause or a chosen fix. Option A names a cause, option C prescribes a solution, and option D is too vague to measure. Only option B states the observable gap with numbers and dates.
  32. The correct answer is C (Cut claims rework from 11 to 5 percent by June 30, with an owner.): A SMART goal is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time bound, and option C carries a baseline, a target, an owner, and a date. The other options ask for vague improvement, add training without a measure, or set no real target and no deadline.
  33. The correct answer is A (The cost of poor quality, which covers the waste created when work fails to meet requirements.): Scrap, rework, warranty claims, and returned goods are all costs created when the process fails to meet requirements, which is the cost of poor quality. The cost of good quality covers prevention and appraisal spending, and total cost of ownership and standard cost of goods describe other accounting views.
  34. The correct answer is A (The Kano model, which splits needs into basic, performance, and delight categories.): The Kano model sorts features by how customers react when a feature is present or absent. A basic need causes dissatisfaction when missing, a performance need scales with how much is delivered, and a delight feature pleases when present but is not expected. The other models cover costs, goal setting, and role assignment.
  35. The correct answer is D (A stakeholder analysis, which lists each party's interest, influence, and likely stance.): A stakeholder analysis identifies everyone who can affect or be affected by the change and records their interest and influence so the team can plan communication.   36.   37. The correct answer is B (The nominal scale, which names categories but allows no order or arithmetic.): Nominal data only labels groups, so counting is allowed but ranking and averaging are not. The ordinal scale allows ranking without equal gaps, the interval scale has equal gaps without a true zero, and the ratio scale has both equal gaps and a true zero. Complaint codes with no natural order are nominal.
  38. The correct answer is A (Continuous data, also called variable data, which can take any value on a scale.): Time measured in seconds can take any value along a scale, so it is continuous or variable data, and continuous data supports capability studies and the usual parametric methods. Attribute, ordinal, and nominal data are counts or labels, which call for other tools and other tests.
  39. The correct answer is A (A gage repeatability and reproducibility study, which separates equipment and appraiser variation.): A gage repeatability and reproducibility study has several appraisers measure the same parts more than once, which separates the variation from the gauge itself, the variation between appraisers, and the part to part variation. Capability, control chart, and experiment studies answer different questions and do not separate those measurement components.
  40. The correct answer is D (Stratified sampling, which draws from each subgroup in proportion to its size.): Stratified sampling splits the population into subgroups and draws a random sample from each one in proportion to its size, which keeps every shift represented. Simple random sampling ignores the subgroups, systematic sampling follows a fixed interval, and convenience sampling takes whatever is easiest to reach.

  43. The correct answer is B (Both Cp and Cpk equal 1, since the process is centered three sigma from each limit.): Cp equals the tolerance width divided by six sigma, which here is 12 divided by 12 and equals 1. The mean of 50 sits 6 units from each limit, which is 3 times the standard deviation of 2, and 6 divided by 6 gives a Cpk of 1 as well, so the process is centered and the two indices agree. The other options misstate either the spread or the centering.

  45. The correct answer is C (Shift the process mean toward the center of the specification range.): When the spread is acceptable and only the centering is off, moving the mean toward the middle of the tolerance increases the distance to the nearest limit and lowers the defect risk at no cost in variation. Widening the limits changes the customer requirement, inspection does not prevent defects, and a smaller sample only weakens the study.
  46. That is a spurious correlation, not evidence that one series causes the other. A causal claim needs more than a matching pattern, and the correlation here is positive rather than negative.
  47. The correct answer is A (The result is statistically significant, so the team rejects the null hypothesis.): When the p value of 0.03 is below the chosen significance level of 0.05, the team rejects the null hypothesis and calls the result statistically significant. The p value does not measure the size of the practical effect, and it is not the probability that the null hypothesis is true. Those are common misreadings, which is why options B, C, and D are wrong.
  48. The correct answer is C (A one way analysis of variance, which compares three or more group means.): Analysis of variance compares the means of three or more groups on a continuous response. With three suppliers and a continuous response, analysis of variance is the right fit.

The correct answer is D (Randomizing the run order so the drift is spread across the factor levels.): Randomization assigns runs to times or positions by chance, so a slow drift such as a warming furnace is spread across the factor levels instead of being confused with one of them. Blocking groups runs that share a known condition, such as a shift or a lot, which does not remove a drift that grows through the day. Replication raises precision but does not separate the drift, and confounding deliberately merges the drift with the effects, which is the problem to avoid.

Option B adds a fixed number per factor, which is not how factorials grow, and option C restates the fraction size instead of the full design.

The correct answer is A (Increase the sample size so a real improvement is easier to detect.): Power is the chance of finding a real effect when one exists, and it rises with a larger sample, a larger effect, or less measurement noise. Shrinking the sample lowers the power. Treating a p-value near alpha as proof of no effect confuses the decision rule with the result, and lowering alpha makes a real effect harder to declare.
